\title{Deep SiMLR: Auditable Multi-Modal Integration for Biomedical
Discovery}
\author{Brian B. Avants \and Nicholas J. Tustison \and James R. Stone}

\bookmarksetup{startatroot}

\chapter*{Abstract}\label{abstract}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Abstract}

\markboth{Abstract}{Abstract}

Integrating multi-modal clinical measurements---such as regional brain
MRI parcellations, neuropsychological batteries, and multi-omic
assays---requires representations that capture shared cross-view signal
while preserving direct attribution to specific anatomical parcels or
molecular markers. Standard deep multi-view networks entangle inputs
across non-linear layers with sample-dependent Jacobians, whereas
Principal Component Analysis (PCA) merges all features into dense,
signed combinations with arbitrary diagonal rotations. We evaluate
\textbf{SiMR} (\emph{Similarity-driven Multi-view Representation}),
which enforces an \textbf{Only Linear Encoder Entrypoint (OLEE)}: each
modality enters through a sparse linear dictionary
\(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}} = \mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m\) regularized on
the approximate Stiefel manifold via \texttt{nsa\_flow} (\(w = 0.5\)),
while subsequent cross-view alignment and decoding can be linear,
autoencoder (\texttt{LEND}), or an invertible normalizing flow
(\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V}).

Across 12 synthetic and real-world cohorts (324 benchmark fits), an
extensive 1,215-fit operating-point evaluation
(\(q \in \{\text{None}, 0.5, 0.8\}\)), and a 1,214-configuration
hyperparameter sweep on Mild Cognitive Impairment in the Alzheimer's
Disease Neuroimaging Initiative (\texttt{ADNI-MCI}, \(N = 1,084\)), we
report four primary findings:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Bipartite clinical supervision:} Aligning structural MRI views
  to an 11-scale cognitive battery during training via a bipartite graph
  (\texttt{Protocol\ B}) raises out-of-sample MRI-only cognitive
  prediction (\texttt{CogPC1}) from \(R^2 = 0.1522 \pm 0.0042\)
  (\texttt{PCA}) to \(0.2520 \pm 0.0075\) for \texttt{LEND} (+65.6\%
  relative gain) and \(0.2377 \pm 0.0149\) for \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V}
  (+56.2\% relative gain), reaching up to \(0.2407\) in sweeps
  (\texttt{SiMLR-Lookahead}).
\item
  \textbf{Subspace fidelity vs.~coordinate axis alignment:} While PCA
  matches linear multi-view models on rotation-invariant linear
  regression probes on low-dimensional subspaces (\(XV\) mean
  \(0.632\)), coordinate-sensitive Random Forest probes reveal that PCA
  singular vectors rotate coordinates diagonally across features: PCA
  drops to \(0.0441\) on ADNI-MCI (vs.~\(0.1969\) for
  \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} and \(0.1692\) for \texttt{LEND}) and to
  \(0.000\) on synthetic weak non-negative ground truth (vs.~\(0.3855\)
  for \texttt{LEND}). Across all 12 datasets, SiMR preserves
  axis-aligned biological separation (\(0.689\) vs.~\(0.589\) for PCA).
\item
  \textbf{Tunable operating points and support recovery:} Setting
  quantile sparseness to canonical operating points---\(q = 0.5\) (50\%
  feature selection for broad discovery) and \(q = 0.8\) (80\% feature
  selection for focal diagnostic biomarker panel design)---recovers
  ground-truth sparse pathways with \(F_1 = 0.991\) (vs.~\(0.333\) for
  PCA) while keeping scale-invariant Stiefel defect strictly bounded
  (\(D \le 0.0009\)).
\item
  \textbf{Regime boundaries:} Across all 12 datasets on the first-layer
  linear probe (\(XV\)), \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} and \texttt{NEDPP} tie for
  \#1 rank (mean rank 3.71), followed by \texttt{LEND} (3.79). On shared
  consensus (\(U\)), \texttt{LEND} ranks \#1 (mean rank 3.33, score
  \(0.696 \pm 0.266\), Canonical Mean Correlation \(CMC = 0.775\)),
  followed by \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} (4.12, score \(0.696 \pm 0.274\),
  \(CMC = 0.794\)). Conversely, on compact two-view tabular cohorts with
  high signal-to-noise ratio (\(p \le 13\)), single-matrix PCA matches
  multi-view models at a fraction of the runtime (\(0.04\text{ s}\)
  vs.~\(2\text{--}9\text{ s}\)).
\end{enumerate}

\bookmarksetup{startatroot}

\chapter{Introduction and Background}\label{introduction-and-background}

\section{Introduction}\label{introduction}

Clinical neuroimaging and multi-omics studies collect multiple
high-dimensional views from each patient, such as regional cortical
volumes, cortical thicknesses, and neuropsychological batteries in the
Alzheimer's Disease Neuroimaging Initiative (ADNI) ({Weiner et al.}
2017; {Jack et al.} 2008), or transcriptomic, copy-number, and proteomic
assays in The Cancer Genome Atlas (TCGA) (The Cancer Genome Atlas
Network 2012; The Cancer Genome Atlas Research Network 2013).
Integrating \(M\) data matrices
\(\mathbf{X}_m \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times p_m}\) (\(m = 1, \dots, M\))
across \(n\) subjects requires a representation that captures cross-view
disease signal while keeping every latent coordinate directly
attributable to specific anatomical regions, genes, or clinical scales
(Avants et al. 2021; {Stone et al.} 2020; {Stone et al.} 2024).

Standard deep multi-view networks---including Deep Canonical Correlation
Analysis (Deep CCA) (Andrew et al. 2013) and Multimodal Variational
Autoencoders (Wu and Goodman 2018)---pass raw inputs through stacked
affine layers and non-linear activations. Because every hidden unit
mixes all input features with sample-dependent derivatives, clinicians
cannot read off which brain regions or transcripts drive a patient's
latent score without relying on post-hoc perturbation surrogates such as
LIME (Ribeiro et al. 2016) or SHAP (Lundberg and Lee 2017). Perturbing
correlated biological features independently evaluates off-manifold
combinations that violate anatomical or genomic covariance (Rudin 2019;
Radulovic 2023). Conversely, classical linear methods such as Principal
Component Analysis (PCA) and Canonical Correlation Analysis (CCA)
(Hotelling 1936) provide constant linear loadings, but dense singular
vectors spread weight across every variable with mixed signs, and
single-matrix concatenation conflates shared disease signal with
view-private variance (Figure~\ref{fig-scientific-overview}).

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\includegraphics[width=1\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{figures/fig-scientific-overview.jpg}

}

\caption{\label{fig-scientific-overview}The Only Linear Encoder
Entrypoint (OLEE) preserves single-feature attribution and eliminates
the black-box transparency penalty while aligning heterogeneous
biomedical views. \textbf{(1) Multi-Modal Clinical Views:} Heterogeneous
measurements \(\mathbf{X}_1, \dots, \mathbf{X}_M\) (structural MRI
cortical thickness, neuropsychological tests, transcriptomic assays;
\(N = 303\text{–}1,084\) across cohorts) are standardized within each
cross-validation training fold. \textbf{(2) Sparse Orthogonal
Entrypoint:} Each view enters strictly through a linear dictionary
\(\mathbf{U}_m = \mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m\), where
\(\mathbf{V}_m \ge 0\) is constrained to the approximate non-negative
Stiefel manifold (\(\mathcal{S}_{p,k}^+\)) via \texttt{nsa\_flow} at
default weight \(w = 0.50\) and quantile sparsity \(q = 0.50\).
\textbf{(3) Shared Patient Consensus:} View projections align around a
shared patient representation via linear consensus (\(\mathbf{U}\)),
non-linear decoding (\texttt{LEND}), or invertible normalizing flows
(\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V}). \textbf{(4) Direct Anatomical \& Biomarker
Attribution:} Because the input boundary is strictly linear, individual
patient risk factors map directly to localized cortical parcels or gene
modules via \(x_{ij} \odot v_{jl}\) without perturbation surrogates.}

\end{figure}%

\subsection{From Classical SiMLR to Non-Linear SiMR and the Linear
Entrypoint}\label{from-classical-simlr-to-non-linear-simr-and-the-linear-entrypoint}

Classical \textbf{SiMLR} (\emph{Similarity-driven Multi-view Linear
Reconstruction}) (Avants et al. 2021) was introduced to bridge
multi-view alignment and sparse linear matrix factorization. Given \(M\)
standardized data matrices
\(\mathbf{X}_m \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times p_m}\), classical SiMLR
estimates a view-specific linear basis
\(\mathbf{V}_m \in \mathbb{R}^{p_m \times k}\) (\(k \ll p_m\)) for each
modality such that the low-dimensional projections
\(\mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m\) align across views around a shared
\(n \times k\) patient consensus \(\mathbf{U}\) while reconstructing
each view linearly.

In this work, we generalize classical SiMLR to \textbf{SiMR}
(\emph{Similarity-driven Multi-view Representation})---dropping the
``Linear'' restriction from the global framework name because the
downstream latent alignment, decoding, or density estimation may be
non-linear, while keeping the input entrypoint strictly linear.
Specifically, every model in the SiMR family enforces an \textbf{Only
Linear Encoder Entrypoint (OLEE)}: \[
\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}} = \mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m, \qquad \mathbf{V}_m \in \mathbb{R}^{p_m \times k}, \quad k \ll p_m.
\] All subsequent operations---whether classical linear consensus
projections (\texttt{SiMLR}, \texttt{SiMLR-LBFGS},
\texttt{SiMLR-LBFGSB}), multi-layer perceptron decoders (\texttt{LEND}),
post-projection encoders (\texttt{NED}, \texttt{NEDPP}), or invertible
normalizing flows (\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V})---operate strictly on the
\(k\)-dimensional linear scores \(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}}\) rather
than raw inputs \(\mathbf{X}_m\). Because the entrypoint is linear, the
input Jacobian of the first-layer scores is constant across all subjects
(\(\nabla_{\mathbf{X}_m}\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}} = \mathbf{V}_m\)), and
minimum-norm counterfactual shifts in feature space follow directly from
\(\mathbf{V}_m^\top\) (Pearl 2009; Wachter et al. 2017).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Approximate Stiefel Retraction
(\texttt{nsa\_flow}), Positivity, and the Weight
\(w \in (0, 1)\)}{Approximate Stiefel Retraction (nsa\_flow), Positivity, and the Weight w \textbackslash in (0, 1)}}\label{approximate-stiefel-retraction-nsa_flow-positivity-and-the-weight-w-in-0-1}

To make the columns of \(\mathbf{V}_m\) interpretable as distinct
anatomical networks or gene modules without rank collapse, we regularize
\(\mathbf{V}_m\) using Non-negative Stiefel Approximating Flow
(\texttt{nsa\_flow}) ({Avants et al.} 2025). Rather than projecting
\(\mathbf{V}_m\) onto the exact Stiefel manifold
\(\mathbf{V}_m^\top \mathbf{V}_m = \mathbf{I}_k\), \texttt{nsa\_flow}
minimizes a dimensionless composite energy controlled by a single convex
weight \(w \in [0, 1]\): \[
E_w(\mathbf{V}_m; \mathbf{V}_{m,0}) = (1 - w)\,\mathcal{L}_{\text{fid}}(\mathbf{V}_m, \mathbf{V}_{m,0}) + w\,D(\mathbf{V}_m),
\] where \(\mathcal{L}_{\text{fid}}\) measures relative distance to the
candidate basis update \(\mathbf{V}_{m,0}\) and \(D(\mathbf{V}_m)\) is
the scale-invariant \textbf{Stiefel defect}: \[
D(\mathbf{V}_m) = \frac{1}{1 - 1/k} \left\| \frac{\mathbf{V}_m^\top \mathbf{V}_m}{\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf{V}_m^\top \mathbf{V}_m)} - \frac{1}{k}\mathbf{I}_k \right\|_F^2, \qquad D(\mathbf{V}_m) \in [0, 1].
\] \(D(\mathbf{V}_m)\) vanishes if and only if the columns of
\(\mathbf{V}_m\) are mutually orthogonal and have equal \(L_2\) norms
(\(\mathbf{V}_m \in \mathbb{R}_{>0} \cdot \mathrm{St}(k, p_m)\)). Values
near zero (\(D \le 10^{-4}\)) indicate mutually perpendicular,
well-conditioned coordinate axes; values exceeding \(0.10\) warn of
column redundancy and effective rank collapse.

This approximate retraction governs optimization whenever non-negativity
(\(\mathbf{V}_m \ge 0\)) is imposed:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Positivity (\(\mathbf{V}_m \ge 0\)) and rank \(k\) govern
  sparsity (\(1 - 1/k\)):} On the unconstrained Stiefel manifold
  (\texttt{positivity="either"}), orthogonal columns remain \(100\%\)
  dense because positive and negative entries cancel in the inner
  product \(\langle \mathbf{v}_{m,l}, \mathbf{v}_{m,r}\rangle = 0\).
  When \(\mathbf{V}_m \ge 0\), two non-negative columns are orthogonal
  if and only if their active feature supports are disjoint
  (\(\operatorname{supp}(\mathbf{v}_{m,l}) \cap \operatorname{supp}(\mathbf{v}_{m,r}) = \emptyset\)).
  Partitioning \(p_m\) features across \(k\) non-overlapping columns
  yields a baseline structural sparsity of at least \(1 - 1/k\)
  (\(50.0\%\) at \(k=2\), \(66.7\%\) at \(k=3\), \(80.0\%\) at \(k=5\),
  and \(91.7\%\) at \(k=12\)).
\item
  \textbf{Balanced operating point (\(q = 0.5\), \(w = 0.5\)):} Below
  \(w \approx 0.20\), the cross-view alignment gradient pulls columns
  together and the basis suffers rank collapse
  (\(\text{EffRank} \to 2.0\) of \(3\) at \(w = 0.01\) on
  \texttt{ADNI-MCI}, with defect \(D = 0.244\)). Conversely, at
  \(w = 1\) the fidelity term \((1 - w)\mathcal{L}_{\text{fid}}\)
  vanishes, leaving matrix scale unconstrained and forcing a hard
  combinatorial partition where shared biological hubs cannot load on
  more than one disease axis ({Avants et al.} 2025). Setting \(w = 0.5\)
  as default (capping hard-manifold requests at \(w_{\max} = 0.95\))
  achieves full effective rank (\(\text{EffRank} = 3.00\),
  \(D \le 6 \times 10^{-4}\)) while preserving continuous optimization
  and overlapping biological pathways.
\item
  \textbf{Row-mean control, ICA initialization, and quantile
  \texttt{sparseness} (\(q = 0.5\) and \(q = 0.8\)):} In neuroimaging
  and multi-omics matrices, regional features covary positively with
  each subject's global amplitude (such as intracranial volume or mean
  cortical thickness, captured by the subject row mean
  \(\bar{x}_{i,m} = \frac{1}{p_m}\sum_{j=1}^{p_m} X_{ij,m}\)). Under
  standard column-centering and PCA initialization without quantile
  thresholding (\texttt{sparseness=None}), the leading singular vector
  \(\mathbf{v}_1\) is an all-positive global size collector
  (\(\mathbf{v}_1 \propto \mathbf{1}_{p_m}\)). Consequently, clamping
  \(\mathbf{v}_1\) to the non-negative orthant zeroes out few entries,
  leaving Component 1 relatively dense (\(35.8\%\) sparsity on
  \texttt{ADNI-MCI} at \(k = 3\)) while squeezing Components
  \(2, \dots, k\) into the remaining features (\(76.6\%\) sparsity). We
  examine two complementary remedies: (1) removing per-subject row means
  inside each training fold and initializing via deterministic spatial
  Independent Component Analysis (ICA), which rotates \(\mathbf{v}_1\)
  away from the global mean and raises Component 1 sparsity to
  \(69.4\%\) (\(67.1\%\) overall, matching \(1 - 1/k = 66.7\%\)); and
  (2) an order-statistic quantile \texttt{sparseness} parameter
  (\(q \in (0, 1)\), defaulting universally to \texttt{0.5}, with
  \texttt{0.5} and \texttt{0.8} as canonical operating points), which
  soft-thresholds each column at its \(q\)-th absolute-weight quantile.
  Setting \(q = 0.50\) raises \(\mathbf{v}_1\) sparsity to \(61.5\%\)
  (\(74.5\%\) overall); \(q = 0.80\) reaches \(81.9\%\) \(\mathbf{v}_1\)
  sparsity (\(85.8\%\) overall); and \(q = 0.85\) achieves \(85.5\%\)
  \(\mathbf{v}_1\) sparsity (\(88.0\%\) overall, exact \(9/62\) active
  DKT regions per component) while improving \texttt{CogPC1} \(R^2\)
  from \(0.1892\) to \(0.1923\).
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Clinical Motivation: Bipartite Cognitive Alignment in
ADNI-MCI}\label{clinical-motivation-bipartite-cognitive-alignment-in-adni-mci}

A primary clinical application of multi-view integration occurs when a
supervisory clinical battery is available during model training but
absent at deployment. In the ADNI Mild Cognitive Impairment cohort
(\texttt{ADNI-MCI}, \(N = 1,084\)), each participant is characterized at
baseline by four views:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Three structural brain MRI views (\(382\) features total):}
  regional gray-matter volumes (\(p_1 = 160\)), hierarchical cortical
  thickness (\(p_2 = 160\)), and native T1-weighted cortical thickness
  (\(p_3 = 62\)).
\item
  \textbf{One neuropsychological battery view (\(p_4 = 11\) scales with
  \(\ge 98\%\) completeness):} \texttt{ADAS11}, \texttt{ADAS13},
  \texttt{ADASQ4}, \texttt{MMSE}, \texttt{CDRSB}, \texttt{TRABSCOR},
  \texttt{FAQ}, and four \texttt{RAVLT} memory sub-scores, residualized
  inside each training split for age, sex, and education via
  Frisch--Waugh--Lovell projection.
\end{itemize}

At clinical deployment (such as screening an MRI scan before scheduling
a full neuropsychological battery), only the three structural MRI views
are observed, and the goal is to predict out-of-sample cognitive
severity (\texttt{CogPC1}, the leading principal component of the 11
cognitive scales, as well as the mean ridge regression \(R^2\) across
all 11 individual scales).

When single-matrix baselines (\texttt{PCA} or \texttt{NSAFlow-PCA}) are
fitted to the three structural MRI views alone, their leading components
capture global brain size and healthy anatomical covariance across the
382 parcels, explaining only \(R^2 = 0.1522 \pm 0.0042\) (\texttt{PCA})
and \(0.1590 \pm 0.0038\) (\texttt{NSAFlow-PCA}) of out-of-sample
\texttt{CogPC1} variance across three cross-validation seeds. In SiMR,
specifying a bipartite training graph (\texttt{Protocol\ B}: aligning
each MRI view \(m \in \{0, 1, 2\}\) directly to the cognitive view
\(m = 3\) during training via
\texttt{path\_graph\ =\ \{0:{[}3{]},\ 1:{[}3{]},\ 2:{[}3{]},\ 3:{[}0,1,2{]}\}},
then withholding View 3 at test time) steers the MRI loading matrices
\(\mathbf{V}_0, \mathbf{V}_1, \mathbf{V}_2\) toward the medial temporal
and entorhinal atrophy patterns associated with cognitive decline.

Using the three MRI views alone at test time, out-of-sample
\texttt{CogPC1} \(R^2\) increases substantially: \texttt{LEND} achieves
\textbf{\(0.2520 \pm 0.0075\)} (+65.6\% relative gain over PCA) and
\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} achieves \textbf{\(0.2377 \pm 0.0149\)} (+56.2\%
over PCA), followed by \texttt{NEDPP} (\(0.2225 \pm 0.0132\)),
\texttt{NED} (\(0.2160 \pm 0.0136\)), \texttt{SiMLR-LBFGS}
(\(0.1686 \pm 0.0109\)), \texttt{SiMLR} (\(0.1684 \pm 0.0218\)), and
\texttt{SiMLR-LBFGSB} (\(0.1682 \pm 0.0124\)) in the 3-seed benchmark,
reaching up to \textbf{\(0.2407\)} (\texttt{SiMLR-Lookahead}) in the
1,214-configuration hyperparameter sweep, while raising the 11-scale
mean \(R^2\) (\texttt{Mean11\_RidgeCV}) from \(0.0813\) (\texttt{PCA})
to \(0.1074\).

\section{Background}\label{background}

\subsection{Subspace Invariance versus Basis Axis
Sensitivity}\label{subspace-invariance-versus-basis-axis-sensitivity}

Evaluating a learned loading matrix
\(\mathbf{V}_m \in \mathbb{R}^{p_m \times k}\) requires distinguishing
the subspace spanned by \(\mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m\) from the
orientation and sparsity of its individual columns:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Subspace invariance under linear regression:} For any
  invertible matrix \(\mathbf{M} \in \mathrm{GL}(k, \mathbb{R})\), the
  transformed scores \(\mathbf{X}_m (\mathbf{V}_m \mathbf{M})\) span the
  same column space as \(\mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m\). Consequently,
  ordinary least squares and unpenalized linear probes depend only on
  \(\operatorname{span}(\mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m)\) and cannot
  distinguish a dense rotated basis from a sparse, localized one
  ({Avants et al.} 2025). This mathematical invariance explains why
  \texttt{PCA} and multi-view SiMR often achieve similar predictive
  accuracy when evaluated on low-dimensional datasets where the leading
  principal subspace already spans the signal.
\item
  \textbf{Axis localization and single-axis interpretability:} In
  clinical biomarker discovery, researchers do not treat coordinates as
  arbitrary rotation vectors. Individual coordinates must align with
  distinct biological pathways (such as entorhinal cortex atrophy
  vs.~frontocingulate network changes). While linear regression is
  rotation-invariant, decision tree ensembles (Random Forests) evaluate
  orthogonal axis splits (\(z_j \le \theta\)). When disease mechanisms
  are diagonally mixed across coordinates (as in PCA), decision trees
  require fragmented split sequences, degrading generalization.
  Coordinate-sensitive Random Forest probes therefore test whether
  learned axes are aligned with isolated biological factors.
\item
  \textbf{Signed contrast splitting and zero lobe crosstalk:} To
  represent bidirectional effects (such as coordinated regional cortical
  thinning and ventricular enlargement) under non-negativity, each basis
  can be split into positive and negative lobes
  \(\mathbf{V}_m = \mathbf{V}_m^+ - \mathbf{V}_m^-\) with
  \(\mathbf{V}_m^+, \mathbf{V}_m^- \ge 0\), where support consolidation
  guarantees zero lobe crosstalk
  (\(\|\mathbf{V}_m^+ \odot \mathbf{V}_m^-\|_1 = 0\)), preventing
  opposing biological loadings from occupying the same feature
  coordinate.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{The Four Evaluation Lenses: A Reader's Guide to the
Evaluation
Probes}\label{the-four-evaluation-lenses-a-readers-guide-to-the-evaluation-probes}

Evaluating multi-view representations requires addressing four distinct
operational questions, each answered by a dedicated evaluation probe
(Table~\ref{tbl-probe-guide}).

  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}

  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}@{}}
\caption{Reader's Guide to the Four Evaluation
Probes.}\label{tbl-probe-guide}\tabularnewline

\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Evaluation Probe
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright

Property Evaluated
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright

\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Evaluation Probe
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Scope \& Model

\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\textbf{Subspace Linear Probe} & Ridge on \(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\) &
Rotation-invariant linear subspace fit & Does the overall subspace span
the disease signal? & Evaluates total information content. PCA and
multi-view models frequently tie here because
\(\operatorname{span}(\mathbf{XVM}) = \operatorname{span}(\mathbf{XV})\). \\
\textbf{Single-Axis Probe} & Random Forest on \(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\) &
Coordinate-sensitive decision tree fit & Do individual coordinates
isolate distinct biological pathways? & Decision trees evaluate axis
splits (\(z_j \le \theta\)). Diagonally mixed bases (PCA) drop in score
(\(0.589\)), while axis-aligned bases (SiMR) preserve performance
(\(0.689\)). \\
\textbf{Patient Consensus Probe} & Ridge / SVM on \(\mathbf{U}\) &
Multi-modal latent consensus fit & Does the shared representation fuse
views without loss? & Tests whether multi-modal fusion creates a compact
patient summary that predicts clinical severity across modalities. \\
\textbf{Support Recovery \& Defect} & \(F_1\) on \(\mathbf{V}\) \&
Defect \(D \in [0, 1]\) & Support Jaccard \(F_1\) and Stiefel defect
\(D\) & Did the model select true active features with non-redundant
columns? & Assesses whether non-zero weights isolate true active markers
(\(F_1 \to 1.0\)) with non-redundant, perpendicular columns
(\(D \to 0\)). \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{Evaluated Architecture
Families}\label{evaluated-architecture-families}

We benchmark nine architectures that share the same data preprocessing
and evaluation protocol:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Single-matrix concatenation baselines (\texttt{PCA},
  \texttt{NSAFlow-PCA}):}

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    \textbf{PCA:} Standard singular value decomposition fitted to the
    concatenated predictor views
    \(\mathbf{X} = [\mathbf{X}_1, \dots, \mathbf{X}_M]\)
    (\(0.043\text{ s}\) mean fit time).
  \item
    \textbf{NSAFlow-PCA:} Single-matrix SVD basis refined via
    \texttt{nsa\_flow} (\(w = 0.5\)), achieving \(25.0\%\) mean sparsity
    in \(0.040\text{ s}\).
  \end{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Linear multi-view OLEE solvers (\texttt{SiMLR},
  \texttt{SiMLR-LBFGS}, \texttt{SiMLR-LBFGSB}):}

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    \textbf{SiMLR:} First-order Armijo gradient descent on the
    cross-view alignment objective with per-sweep \texttt{nsa\_flow}
    retraction (\(0.556\text{ s}\)).
  \item
    \textbf{SiMLR-LBFGS:} Two-loop quasi-Newton optimization with
    \texttt{nsa\_flow} retraction (\(\text{Support } F_1 = 0.966\),
    \(3.543\text{ s}\)).
  \item
    \textbf{SiMLR-LBFGSB:} Bound-constrained L-BFGS-B optimization
    enforcing \(\mathbf{V}_m \ge 0\) directly inside the quasi-Newton
    update when positivity is active (\(\text{Support } F_1 = 0.991\),
    \(3.910\text{ s}\)).
  \end{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Non-linear decoder and encoder-decoder SiMR models
  (\texttt{LEND}, \texttt{NED}, \texttt{NEDPP}):}

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    \textbf{LEND (Linear Encoder, Non-linear Decoder):} Projects each
    view strictly linearly
    (\(\mathbf{Z}_m = \mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m\)) and reconstructs
    \(\mathbf{X}_m\) through a multi-layer perceptron decoder
    \(g_m(\mathbf{Z}_m)\) (\(3.756\text{ s}\)).
  \item
    \textbf{NED (Non-linear Encoder-Decoder):} Places an MLP encoder on
    top of the linear projection
    (\(\mathbf{Z}_m = f_m(\mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m)\)) paired with an
    MLP decoder (\(3.021\text{ s}\)).
  \item
    \textbf{NEDPP (Shared and Private Subspaces):} Extends \texttt{NED}
    by splitting latent coordinates into shared consensus and
    view-private partitions with a cross-covariance penalty
    (\(3.380\text{ s}\)).
  \end{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Invertible normalizing flow downstream of OLEE
  (\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V}):}

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    \textbf{Flow-SiMR-V:} Composes the linear entrypoint
    \(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}} = \mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m\) with
    volume-preserving RealNVP affine coupling layers
    \(f_{\text{flow},m}: \mathbb{R}^k \to \mathbb{R}^k\) trained with
    detached-prior subspace decoupling (\(9.563\text{ s}\)).
  \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

In the following chapter, we formalize the variational objective,
Riemannian retraction mechanics, and 3-stage support-masked flow polish
that govern these architectures.

\bookmarksetup{startatroot}

\chapter{Methods}\label{methods}

\textbf{SiMR} (\emph{Similarity-driven Multi-view Representation})
estimates a low-dimensional representation across \(M\) views
\(\mathbf{X}_m \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times p_m}\) (\(m = 1, \dots, M\))
collected on \(n\) subjects, generalizing classical Similarity-driven
Multi-view Linear Reconstruction (\textbf{SiMLR}) (Avants et al. 2021)
to support both linear and non-linear latent alignment. Every
architecture in the family shares a common structural boundary---the
Only Linear Encoder Entrypoint (OLEE)---and minimizes a generic
composite objective combining within-view reconstruction, cross-view
alignment, and approximate Stiefel regularization via \texttt{nsa\_flow}
({Avants et al.} 2025). Table~\ref{tbl-notation} summarizes the
mathematical notation.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5000}}@{}}
\caption{Core mathematical notation for the SiMR
framework.}\label{tbl-notation}\tabularnewline
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Term
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Meaning
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endfirsthead
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Term
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Meaning
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\(\mathbf{X}_m \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times p_m}\) & Standardized data
matrix for view \(m \in \{1, \dots, M\}\) across \(n\) subjects and
\(p_m\) features \\
\(\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{m,\text{row}} \in \mathbb{R}^n\) & Per-subject row
mean vector \(\bar{x}_{i,m} = \frac{1}{p_m}\sum_{j=1}^{p_m} X_{ij,m}\),
capturing global view amplitude (e.g., mean cortical thickness or brain
size) \\
\(\mathbf{V}_m \in \mathbb{R}^{p_m \times k}\) & First-layer linear
loading matrix (OLEE basis) for view \(m\), with latent rank
\(k \ll p_m\) \\
\(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}} = \mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times k}\)
& Linear entrypoint scores for view \(m\), with constant input
derivative
\(\nabla_{\mathbf{X}_m}\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}} = \mathbf{V}_m\) \\
\(\mathbf{Z}_m = f_m(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}}) \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times k}\)
& Post-entrypoint latent embedding (\(f_m = \mathrm{id}\) for linear
\texttt{SiMLR} and \texttt{LEND} models; MLP or flow for
\texttt{NED}/\texttt{NEDPP}/\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V}) \\
\(g_m : \mathbb{R}^k \to \mathbb{R}^{p_m}\) & View-specific decoder
mapping latent coordinates back to the ambient feature space
\(\mathbb{R}^{p_m}\) \\
\(\mathbf{U}_m \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times k}\) & Cross-view consensus
target for view \(m\), formed from neighbor views \(\mathcal{N}(m)\) in
alignment graph \(\mathcal{G}\) \\
\(w \in [0, 1]\) & Convex weight in \texttt{nsa\_flow} balancing anchor
fidelity \((1-w)\) against Stiefel orthogonality defect \(w\) (default
\(w = 0.5\)) \\
\(D(\mathbf{V}_m) \in [0, 1]\) & Normalized Stiefel defect measuring
distance of the trace-normalized Gram matrix
\(\mathbf{V}_m^\top \mathbf{V}_m / \operatorname{tr}(\mathbf{V}_m^\top \mathbf{V}_m)\)
from \(\frac{1}{k}\mathbf{I}_k\) \\
\end{longtable}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Generic Multi-View
Objective}\label{generic-multi-view-objective}

Given \(M\) views \(\{\mathbf{X}_m\}_{m=1}^M\) and an alignment graph
\(\mathcal{G} = (\{1, \dots, M\}, \mathcal{E})\), SiMR minimizes the
generic multi-view objective:
\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-generic-objective}{
\min_{\{\mathbf{V}_m, f_m, g_m\}_{m=1}^M} \sum_{m=1}^M \Big[ \mathcal{L}_{\text{recon}}\!\big(\mathbf{X}_m,\, g_m(\mathbf{Z}_m)\big) + \lambda_{\text{sim}} \, \alpha_m \, \mathcal{L}_{\text{align}}\!\big(\mathbf{Z}_m,\, \mathbf{U}_m\big) + E_w\!\big(\mathbf{V}_m;\, \mathbf{V}_{m,0}\big) \Big] + \mathcal{R}\!\big(\{\mathbf{Z}_m\}\big),
}\end{equation} where
\(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}} = \mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m\) is the linear
entrypoint projection, \(\mathbf{Z}_m = f_m(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}})\)
is the latent representation of view \(m\),
\(\mathbf{U}_m = \operatorname{Consensus}(\{\mathbf{Z}_j\}_{j \in \mathcal{N}(m)})\)
is the cross-view target aggregated over graph neighbors
\(\mathcal{N}(m)\), \(\alpha_m \ge 0\) is the modality weight
(\(\sum_m \alpha_m = 1\)), \(E_w(\mathbf{V}_m; \mathbf{V}_{m,0})\) is
the \texttt{nsa\_flow} approximate Stiefel regularizer, and
\(\mathcal{R}(\{\mathbf{Z}_m\})\) denotes optional latent regularizers
(such as the shared-private orthogonality penalty in \texttt{NEDPP} or
the detached Gaussian prior log-likelihood in \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V}).

Table~\ref{tbl-method-instantiations} summarizes how each of the nine
benchmarked architectures instantiates
Equation~\ref{eq-generic-objective}.

\subsection{Linear Attribution and Counterfactual Recourse at the OLEE
Boundary}\label{linear-attribution-and-counterfactual-recourse-at-the-olee-boundary}

Restricting the first layer of every view to
\(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}} = \mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m\) guarantees two
mathematical properties:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Constant per-feature attribution:} For subject \(i\) and
  latent coordinate \(l \in \{1, \dots, k\}\), the entrypoint score is
  \(Z_{m,il} = \sum_{j=1}^{p_m} X_{m,ij} V_{m,jl}\). The derivative with
  respect to \(\mathbf{X}_m\) is identically \(\mathbf{V}_m\) for every
  subject. Patient-level feature attributions are given directly by the
  Hadamard product \(\mathbf{x}_{m,i} \odot \mathbf{v}_{m,l}\) without
  post-hoc perturbation sampling.
\item
  \textbf{Closed-form minimum-norm counterfactual shift:} For a
  unit-column-norm basis with
  \(\mathbf{V}_m^\top \mathbf{V}_m \approx \mathbf{I}_k\), the
  minimum-\(L_2\)-norm feature shift
  \(\delta \mathbf{x}_m^* \in \mathbb{R}^{p_m}\) required to produce a
  latent change \(\Delta \mathbf{z}_m \in \mathbb{R}^k\)
  (\(\delta \mathbf{x}_m \mathbf{V}_m = \Delta \mathbf{z}_m\)) is the
  linear projection
  \(\delta \mathbf{x}_m^* = \Delta \mathbf{z}_m (\mathbf{V}_m^\top \mathbf{V}_m)^{-1} \mathbf{V}_m^\top \approx \Delta \mathbf{z}_m \mathbf{V}_m^\top\).
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Approximate Stiefel Retraction via
\texttt{nsa\_flow}}{Approximate Stiefel Retraction via nsa\_flow}}\label{approximate-stiefel-retraction-via-nsa_flow}

When an outer optimization step proposes a candidate loading matrix
\(\mathbf{V}_{m,0} \in \mathbb{R}^{p_m \times k}\), \texttt{nsa\_flow}
minimizes the composite energy:
\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-nsa-energy}{
\min_{\mathbf{V}_m \in \mathcal{C}} E_w(\mathbf{V}_m; \mathbf{V}_{m,0}) = (1 - w)\,\mathcal{L}_{\text{fid}}(\mathbf{V}_m, \mathbf{V}_{m,0}) + w\,D(\mathbf{V}_m),
}\end{equation} where
\(\mathcal{C} = \{\mathbf{V} \in \mathbb{R}^{p_m \times k} : \mathbf{V} \ge 0\}\)
when non-negativity is active (\texttt{positivity="positive"}, or
\(\mathbb{R}^{p_m \times k}\) when unconstrained
\texttt{positivity="either"}), and \(w \in [0, 1]\) is a convex
balancing weight.

\subsection{Dimensionless Energy
Terms}\label{dimensionless-energy-terms}

Both terms in Equation~\ref{eq-nsa-energy} are dimensionless and bounded
on \([0, 1]\):

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Fidelity term (\(\mathcal{L}_{\text{fid}}\)):} Measures the
  relative Frobenius distance to the candidate update
  \(\mathbf{V}_{m,0}\), \[
  \mathcal{L}_{\text{fid}}(\mathbf{V}_m, \mathbf{V}_{m,0}) = \frac{\|\mathbf{V}_m - \mathbf{V}_{m,0}\|_F^2}{\|\mathbf{V}_{m,0}\|_F^2},
  \] which equals \(0\) at \(\mathbf{V}_m = \mathbf{V}_{m,0}\) and \(1\)
  at \(\mathbf{V}_m = \mathbf{0}\).
\item
  \textbf{Normalized Stiefel defect (\(D(\mathbf{V}_m)\)):} Let
  \(\mathbf{S}_m = \mathbf{V}_m^\top \mathbf{V}_m\),
  \(t_m = \operatorname{tr}(\mathbf{S}_m) = \|\mathbf{V}_m\|_F^2\), and
  \(\mathbf{G}_m = \mathbf{S}_m / t_m\) be the trace-normalized Gram
  matrix. The normalized Stiefel defect is
  \begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-stiefel-defect}{
  D(\mathbf{V}_m) = \frac{1}{1 - 1/k}\left\|\mathbf{G}_m - \frac{1}{k}\mathbf{I}_k\right\|_F^2 = \frac{1}{1 - 1/k}\Bigg[\underbrace{\sum_{l \neq r} G_{m,lr}^2}_{\text{decorrelation}} + \underbrace{\sum_{l=1}^k \Big(G_{m,ll} - \frac{1}{k}\Big)^2}_{\text{norm balance}}\Bigg].
  }\end{equation} \(D(\mathbf{V}_m)\) vanishes if and only if the
  columns of \(\mathbf{V}_m\) are mutually orthogonal and have equal
  norms
  (\(\mathbf{V}_m \in \mathbb{R}_{>0} \cdot \mathrm{St}(k, p_m)\)).
  Because
  \(D(\mathbf{V}_m) = \frac{1}{1 - 1/k}\big(\frac{1}{\operatorname{EffRank}(\mathbf{V}_m)} - \frac{1}{k}\big)\),
  any rank-deficient matrix of rank \(r < k\) incurs a strictly positive
  floor \(D(\mathbf{V}_m) \ge \frac{1/r - 1/k}{1 - 1/k}\). By Euler's
  identity for degree-zero homogeneous functions,
  \(\langle \nabla_{\mathbf{V}_m} D(\mathbf{V}_m), \mathbf{V}_m \rangle = 0\)
  identically, so \(D(\mathbf{V}_m)\) rotates and balances columns
  without modifying total matrix scale \(\|\mathbf{V}_m\|_F\).
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Structural Sparsity and Weight Selection
(\(w = 0.5\))}{Structural Sparsity and Weight Selection (w = 0.5)}}\label{structural-sparsity-and-weight-selection-w-0.5}

For a non-negative matrix \(\mathbf{V}_m \ge 0\), two columns
\(\mathbf{v}_{m,l}\) and \(\mathbf{v}_{m,r}\) satisfy
\(\langle \mathbf{v}_{m,l}, \mathbf{v}_{m,r} \rangle = 0\) if and only
if their non-zero supports are disjoint:
\(\operatorname{supp}(\mathbf{v}_{m,l}) \cap \operatorname{supp}(\mathbf{v}_{m,r}) = \emptyset\).
Consequently:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{The \(1 - 1/k\) structural sparsity bound:} Under
  \(\mathbf{V}_m \ge 0\) and \(D(\mathbf{V}_m) \to 0\), each feature can
  load on at most one of the \(k\) columns. Across \(k p_m\) entries, at
  most \(p_m\) entries are non-zero, yielding a theoretical baseline
  sparsity of
  \begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-k-sparsity}{
  \operatorname{Sparsity}(\mathbf{V}_m) \ge 1 - \frac{p_m}{k p_m} = 1 - \frac{1}{k},
  }\end{equation} which evaluates to \(50.0\%\) at \(k = 2\), \(66.7\%\)
  at \(k = 3\), \(80.0\%\) at \(k = 5\), and \(91.7\%\) at \(k = 12\).
  When unconstrained signed bases are fitted
  (\texttt{positivity="either"}), columns satisfy
  \(\mathbf{V}_m^\top \mathbf{V}_m = \mathbf{I}_k\) via signed
  cancellation with \(0\%\) sparsity.
\item
  \textbf{Weight behavior (\(w\)):} When \(w < 0.20\), the cross-view
  alignment gradient dominates orthogonality, collapsing columns onto
  collinear directions (\(\operatorname{EffRank}(\mathbf{V}_m) \to 2.0\)
  of \(3\) at \(w = 0.01\) on \texttt{ADNI-MCI}, with defect
  \(D = 0.244\)). At \(w = 1\), the fidelity term
  \((1 - w)\mathcal{L}_{\text{fid}}\) vanishes, leaving scale
  unconstrained and enforcing a rigid combinatorial partition where
  shared biological hubs cannot participate in multiple pathways.
  Setting \(w = 0.5\) as default (capping hard-manifold requests at
  \(w_{\max} = 0.95\)) achieves full effective rank
  (\(\operatorname{EffRank} = 3.00\), \(D \le 6 \times 10^{-4}\)) in
  \(O(p_m k^2)\) operations per iteration without inner-loop SVD or QR
  factorizations.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{The 3-Stage Support-Masked Polish and Sign-Gauge
Resolution}\label{the-3-stage-support-masked-polish-and-sign-gauge-resolution}

When quantile sparseness \(q\) is specified, \texttt{nsa\_flow} executes
a 3-stage support-masked Riemannian polish
(Figure~\ref{fig-nsa-flow-polish-pipeline}):

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Stage 1 (Full-Support Riemannian Flow):} Solves an
  unconstrained Riemannian flow minimizing
  \(E_w(\mathbf{Y}; \mathbf{Y}_0)\) on the Stiefel manifold across all
  \(p_m\) features, identifying the dominant multi-modal spectral
  subspace.
\item
  \textbf{Stage 2 (Support Masking \& Per-Column Sign-Gauge
  Resolution):} For each column \(\mathbf{y}_l \in \mathbb{R}^{p_m}\)
  and target active count
  \(s_m = \max(1, \operatorname{round}((1 - q_m)p_m))\), the
  order-statistic threshold \(\tau_{m,l} = |y_{(s_m+1),l}|\) is
  calculated. Under \texttt{only\_if\_denser=True}, if a column already
  contains more than \(q_m\) zeros, its natural zeros are retained.
  Otherwise, a binary support mask
  \(\mathbf{M}_m \in \{0, 1\}^{p_m \times k}\) is defined by
  \(M_{jl} = \mathbf{1}(|y_{jl}| > \tau_{m,l})\).

  Prior to non-negative projection or clamping (\(\mathbf{V} \ge 0\)),
  the per-column sign gauge is resolved by evaluating positive
  vs.~negative \(L_2\) mass survivor:
  \begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-sign-gauge}{
  \mathbf{v}_{m,l} \leftarrow -\mathbf{v}_{m,l} \quad \text{if } \|\max(0, -\mathbf{v}_{m,l})\|_2 > \|\max(0, \mathbf{v}_{m,l})\|_2.
  }\end{equation} This reflection ensures that the dominant biological
  activation is oriented positively before non-negative clamping
  \(\mathbf{V}_m \leftarrow \max(0, \mathbf{V}_m)\).
\item
  \textbf{Stage 3 (Masked Stiefel Polish):} With the support mask
  \(\mathbf{M}_m\) fixed, \texttt{nsa\_flow} executes Riemannian
  optimization with gradients pinned strictly to the active coordinates
  (\(\nabla_{\mathbf{Y}} E \odot \mathbf{M}_m\)). This step restores
  column orthogonality and norm balance without leaking weight into
  zeroed background features, ensuring \(D(\mathbf{V}_m) \le 10^{-4}\)
  alongside exact feature sparsity.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\includegraphics[width=37.74in,height=4.13in]{02_methods_files/figure-latex/mermaid-figure-1.png}

}

\caption{\label{fig-nsa-flow-polish-pipeline}A 3-stage support-masked
Riemannian polish decouples direction finding from sparsity enforcement
to eliminate \(L_1\) shrinkage bias. Stage 1 solves unconstrained
Riemannian flow (\(w = 0.50\)) to identify the global spectral subspace;
Stage 2 applies per-column sign-gauge resolution
(\(v_j \leftarrow -v_j\)) before quantile order-statistic masking
(\(\tau_l = |V_{(s+1),l}|\)); Stage 3 polishes the pinned active support
on the Stiefel manifold to restore exact column orthogonality
(\(D \le 10^{-4}\)) with zero background weight leakage.}

\end{figure}%

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Controlling Component 1 Sparsity: Row-Mean
Control, Deterministic ICA, and Quantile
\texttt{sparseness}}{Controlling Component 1 Sparsity: Row-Mean Control, Deterministic ICA, and Quantile sparseness}}\label{controlling-component-1-sparsity-row-mean-control-deterministic-ica-and-quantile-sparseness}

When applying non-negative basis learning to neuroimaging or multi-omics
matrices under standard column-centering and PCA initialization, each
subject possesses a non-zero row mean across features:
\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-row-mean-decomp}{
\bar{x}_{i,m} = \frac{1}{p_m}\sum_{j=1}^{p_m} X_{ij,m}, \qquad \mathbf{X}_m = \bar{\mathbf{x}}_{m,\text{row}}\mathbf{1}_{p_m}^\top + \tilde{\mathbf{X}}_m, \quad \tilde{\mathbf{X}}_m \mathbf{1}_{p_m} = \mathbf{0}.
}\end{equation} In structural brain MRI, \(\bar{x}_{i,m}\) represents
subject \(i\)'s mean cortical thickness or parenchymal volume across all
\(p_m\) anatomical parcels. Because regional volumes and thicknesses
covary positively across the cortex, the sample covariance matrix
\(\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{X}_m^\top \mathbf{X}_m\) has predominantly positive
entries. By the Perron--Frobenius theorem, its leading eigenvector
\(\mathbf{v}_{m,1}^{\text{PCA}}\) is dense and strictly positive
(\(\mathbf{v}_{m,1}^{\text{PCA}} \approx \mathbf{1}_{p_m}/\sqrt{p_m}\)),
while subsequent eigenvectors
\(\mathbf{v}_{m,2}^{\text{PCA}}, \dots, \mathbf{v}_{m,k}^{\text{PCA}}\)
contain positive and negative entries to satisfy orthogonality.

Clamping PCA initialization to \(\mathbf{V}_m \ge 0\) leaves
\(\mathbf{v}_{m,1}\) dense (\(35.8\%\) sparsity on \texttt{ADNI-MCI} at
\(k = 3\)) while forcing Components \(2, \dots, k\) into the remaining
parcels (\(76.6\%\) sparsity). Because \(D(\mathbf{V}_m)\) in
Equation~\ref{eq-stiefel-defect} penalizes only between-column inner
products and column-norm imbalance, any partition of features into \(k\)
disjoint subsets of unit norm achieves \(D(\mathbf{V}_m) = 0\),
including asymmetric partitions.

We examine three complementary mechanisms in \texttt{PySiMLR} to control
Component 1 density:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{In-fold row-mean control (double-centering):} Projecting each
  matrix onto the orthogonal complement of \(\mathbf{1}_{p_m}\)
  (\(\tilde{\mathbf{X}}_m = \mathbf{X}_m - \bar{\mathbf{x}}_{m,\text{row}}\mathbf{1}_{p_m}^\top\))
  removes the rank-1 global subject mode prior to optimization.
\item
  \textbf{Deterministic spatial ICA initialization
  (\texttt{initialization\_type="ica"}):} Given deterministic singular
  vectors \(\mathbf{V}_{m,\text{SVD}} \in \mathbb{R}^{p_m \times k}\),
  parallel \texttt{FastICA} (\texttt{fun="logcosh"}, seeded with
  \(\mathbf{W}_0 = \mathbf{I}_k\)) rotates the basis toward spatially
  localized directions before retraction. Combining row-mean removal and
  ICA initialization equalizes sparsity across columns
  (\(66\%\text{--}69\%\) at \(k=3\)), though discarding global atrophy
  covariance reduces out-of-sample cognitive prediction on
  \texttt{ADNI-MCI}.
\item
  \textbf{Quantile \texttt{sparseness} operator (\(q \in (0, 1)\),
  default \(0.5\), operating points \(0.5\) and \(0.8\)):} Following
  Avants et al. (2021), we define three primary operating points:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    \textbf{\(q = \text{None}\) (Unsparsified Baseline):} Relies
    strictly on \texttt{nsa\_flow} Stiefel regularization and
    non-negativity to enforce structural sparsity (\(1 - 1/k\)).
  \item
    \textbf{\(q = 0.5\) (Balanced Discovery):} Soft-thresholds the
    lowest \(50\%\) of weights per column, eliminating diffuse
    background noise while preserving secondary pathway hubs.
  \item
    \textbf{\(q = 0.8\) (Focal Diagnostic Biomarker Panel Design):}
    Soft-thresholds \(80\%\) of features per column, isolating compact
    panels of the top \(20\%\) focal biomarkers (such as 9 DKT cortical
    parcels or 20 diagnostic genes) for translational clinical assays.
  \end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Cross-View Consensus Topologies and Bipartite Clinical
Alignment}\label{cross-view-consensus-topologies-and-bipartite-clinical-alignment}

The cross-view alignment term
\(\mathcal{L}_{\text{align}}(\mathbf{Z}_m, \mathbf{U}_m)\) pulls each
view's latent representation
\(\mathbf{Z}_m \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times k}\) toward a view-specific
consensus target \(\mathbf{U}_m\) constructed from its neighbor set
\(\mathcal{N}(m)\) in graph \(\mathcal{G}\). Prior to averaging across
views, columns are sign-aligned (\texttt{align\_column\_signs}) against
a running reference, followed by Gram--Schmidt, SVD, or 10-step
Newton--Schulz orthogonalization.

SiMR supports three consensus topologies:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Pooled star topology (\texttt{topology="star"}):} Every view
  aligns to the global consensus of all \(M\) views,
  \(\mathcal{N}(m) = \{1, \dots, M\}\).
\item
  \textbf{Leave-one-out topology (\texttt{topology="loo"}):} View \(m\)
  aligns strictly against the consensus of the remaining \(M - 1\)
  views, \(\mathcal{N}(m) = \{j \neq m\}\), preventing a
  high-dimensional view from dominating via self-correlation.
\item
  \textbf{Directed and bipartite graph topology
  (\texttt{topology="graph"}):} In clinical cohorts where \(M - 1\)
  predictor views \(\{\mathbf{X}_1, \dots, \mathbf{X}_{M-1}\}\) (e.g.,
  three structural MRI views in \texttt{ADNI-MCI}) are accompanied
  during training by a supervisory clinical or cognitive view
  \(\mathbf{X}_M\) (e.g., the 11-scale cognitive battery), aligning the
  MRI views with each other allows global brain size and healthy aging
  covariance to dominate the leading components. Specifying the
  bipartite neighbor sets
  \begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-bipartite-graph}{
  \mathcal{N}(m) = \{M\} \quad (m = 1, \dots, M-1), \qquad \mathcal{N}(M) = \{1, \dots, M-1\}
  }\end{equation} routes all cross-view alignment during training
  between the imaging views and the cognitive battery
  (\texttt{Protocol\ B}). At test time, View \(M\) is withheld
  (\texttt{eval\_view\_indices} \(= \{1, \dots, M-1\}\)) and
  out-of-sample predictions are formed strictly from the test imaging
  views: \[
  \mathbf{Z}_{\text{test},\text{lin}} = \big[\mathbf{X}_{1,\text{test}}\mathbf{V}_1, \dots, \mathbf{X}_{M-1,\text{test}}\mathbf{V}_{M-1}\big], \qquad \hat{\mathbf{U}}_{\text{test}} = \frac{1}{M - 1}\sum_{m=1}^{M-1} f_m(\mathbf{X}_{m,\text{test}}\mathbf{V}_m).
  \]
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Non-Linear Decoding (\texttt{LEND}) and
Detached-Prior Normalizing Flows
(\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V})}{Non-Linear Decoding (LEND) and Detached-Prior Normalizing Flows (Flow-SiMR-V)}}\label{non-linear-decoding-lend-and-detached-prior-normalizing-flows-flow-simr-v}

To capture non-linear relationships between the shared latent state and
the observed measurements while preserving the linear input boundary
\(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}} = \mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m\), SiMR provides
two primary non-linear extensions:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Linear Encoder, Non-linear Decoder (\texttt{LEND}):} Keeps the
  encoder strictly linear (\(f_m = \mathrm{id}\), so
  \(\mathbf{Z}_m = \mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m\)) and places a multi-layer
  perceptron in the decoder \(g_m : \mathbb{R}^k \to \mathbb{R}^{p_m}\).
  Because the encoder has only \(p_m k\) parameters regularized by
  \(E_w(\mathbf{V}_m)\), \texttt{LEND} avoids the high-dimensional
  encoder overfitting that occurs when MLP layers are inserted inside
  the encoder (\texttt{NED} and \texttt{NEDPP}) when \(p_m \gg n\).
\item
  \textbf{Bijective Normalizing Flow Downstream of OLEE
  (\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V}):} Applies an invertible RealNVP affine coupling
  flow \(f_{\text{flow},m} : \mathbb{R}^k \to \mathbb{R}^k\) to the
  linear projection
  \(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}} = \mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m\), with exact
  inverse reconstruction
  \(\hat{\mathbf{X}}_m = f_{\text{flow},m}^{-1}(\mathbf{Z}_m)\mathbf{V}_m^\top\).
\end{itemize}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Detached-Prior Subspace Decoupling in
\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V}}{Detached-Prior Subspace Decoupling in Flow-SiMR-V}}\label{detached-prior-subspace-decoupling-in-flow-simr-v}

If the standard Gaussian prior negative log-likelihood \[
\mathcal{L}_{\text{NLL}}(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}}; f_{\text{flow},m}) = \frac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^n \|f_{\text{flow},m}(\mathbf{z}_{m,\text{lin},i})\|_2^2 - \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \log\left|\det \frac{\partial f_{\text{flow},m}}{\partial \mathbf{z}_{m,\text{lin}}}(\mathbf{z}_{m,\text{lin},i})\right|
\] is backpropagated directly into the rectangular basis
\(\mathbf{V}_m \in \mathbb{R}^{p_m \times k}\) (\(k \ll p_m\)),
minimizing
\(\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{Z}_m\|_2^2 \approx \frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf{V}_m^\top \mathbf{X}_m^\top \mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m)\)
with respect to \(\mathbf{V}_m\) rotates \(\mathbf{V}_m\) onto the
trailing (smallest-eigenvalue) noise eigenvectors of
\(\mathbf{X}_m^\top \mathbf{X}_m\), because the Jacobian determinant
accounts only for volume changes inside \(\mathbb{R}^k\).
\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} prevents this collapse by detaching
\(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}}\) at the input to the Gaussian prior
loss---evaluating
\(\mathcal{L}_{\text{NLL}}\big(f_{\text{flow},m}(\operatorname{sg}(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}}))\big)\)
where \(\operatorname{sg}(\cdot)\) is the stop-gradient operator---so
that \(\mathbf{V}_m\) is updated exclusively by cross-view alignment and
reconstruction fidelity while \(f_{\text{flow},m}\) warps the resulting
\(k\)-dimensional coordinates toward the Gaussian prior.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Evaluation Protocol: The Three Probes and Statistical
Inference}\label{evaluation-protocol-the-three-probes-and-statistical-inference}

All benchmark experiments follow leak-free evaluation rules:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{In-fold preprocessing:} Feature standardization
  (\texttt{StandardScaler}), column completeness filtering
  (\(\ge 98\%\)), demographic residualization (\texttt{AGE},
  \texttt{PTGENDER}, \texttt{PTEDUCAT} on \texttt{ADNI-MCI}), and
  optional per-subject row-mean removal are fitted strictly inside each
  training split and applied to the held-out test split.
\item
  \textbf{Evaluation Probes and Ground-Truth Recovery:} We evaluate
  models under four complementary operational lenses:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    \textbf{Probe 1: Subspace Linear Probe on \(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\)
    (Rotation-Invariant):} Ridge regression predicting held-out targets
    from concatenated first-layer scores
    \([\mathbf{X}_{1,\text{test}}\mathbf{V}_1, \dots, \mathbf{X}_{M,\text{test}}\mathbf{V}_M]\).
    Because
    \(\operatorname{span}(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\mathbf{M}) = \operatorname{span}(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V})\),
    this probe measures whether the learned subspace captures the
    overall disease variance.
  \item
    \textbf{Probe 2: Single-Axis Random Forest Probe on
    \(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\) (Coordinate-Sensitive):} Random Forest
    decision tree ensemble predicting held-out targets from
    \(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\). Decision trees split on single coordinates
    (\(z_j \le \theta\)). When biological factors are diagonally rotated
    across multiple axes (as in PCA), Random Forest performance drops
    precipitously. This probe directly verifies whether learned
    coordinates are aligned with true biological axes.
  \item
    \textbf{Probe 3: Shared Patient Consensus Probe on \(\mathbf{U}\):}
    Ridge or SVM classifier predicting held-out targets from the fused
    \(k\)-dimensional patient consensus embedding
    \(\hat{\mathbf{U}}_{\text{test}}\), evaluating multi-modal
    compression.
  \item
    \textbf{Ground-Truth Support Recovery \& Defect:} On synthetic
    regimes with known ground truth \((\mathbf{U}^*, \mathbf{V}_m^*)\),
    we evaluate Canonical Mean Correlation (\(CMC\) between
    \(\hat{\mathbf{U}}\) and \(\mathbf{U}^*\)), sparse support Jaccard
    \(F_1\), and scale-invariant Stiefel defect
    \(D(\mathbf{V}_m) \in [0, 1]\).
  \end{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Block-level non-parametric inference:} Following Demšar
  (2006), multi-seed replicates are averaged within each
  \((\text{Dataset}, \text{Model})\) cell
  (\texttt{build\_ranking\_blocks} in
  \texttt{src/pysimlr/benchmarks/deep\_ranking.py}) so that repeated
  seeds are treated as replicates of a dataset rather than independent
  tasks. Across the \(B = 12\) independent dataset blocks and \(k = 9\)
  models, we report the Friedman \(\chi_F^2\) test, the Iman--Davenport
  \(F_F\) statistic (Iman and Davenport 1980), the two-tailed Nemenyi
  Critical Difference (\(CD = 3.468\) at \(\alpha = 0.05\)) (Nemenyi
  1963), and pairwise Wilcoxon signed-rank tests with Holm step-down
  adjustment (Holm 1979) alongside exact sign-flip permutation
  \(p\)-values.
\end{enumerate}

\bookmarksetup{startatroot}

\chapter{Experiments}\label{experiments}

We evaluate the nine architectures across 12 multi-view datasets (six
synthetic generative regimes with known ground truth and six real
clinical, multi-omics, and pattern-recognition cohorts) over three
independent random seeds (\([42, 43, 44]\), totaling
\(12 \times 3 \times 9 = 324\) model fits), supplemented by a
1,214-configuration hyperparameter sweep on the four-view
\texttt{ADNI-MCI} neuroimaging cohort (\(N = 1,084\)). Every table and
statistic in this chapter is computed directly at build time from the
cached benchmark outputs
\texttt{paper/results\_cache/deep\_ranking\_benchmark.csv} and
\texttt{paper/results\_cache/adni\_mci\_1214\_grid\_sweep.csv}.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Experimental Design, Real Cohorts, and Synthetic
Regimes}\label{experimental-design-real-cohorts-and-synthetic-regimes}

\subsection{Real Clinical, Multi-Omics, and Multi-View
Cohorts}\label{real-clinical-multi-omics-and-multi-view-cohorts}

All real-world cohorts are evaluated using stratified or random 70/30
train/test splits across seeds \(42, 43,\) and \(44\), with feature
standardization (\texttt{StandardScaler}), column completeness filtering
(\(\ge 98\%\)), and demographic covariate residualization fitted
strictly inside each training split:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{ADNI-MCI Neurodegeneration (\texttt{ADNI-MCI}, \(N = 1,084\),
  4 views: \(p = [160, 160, 62, 11]\)):} Baseline data from
  \(N = 1,084\) participants with Mild Cognitive Impairment in ADNI
  ({Weiner et al.} 2017; {Jack et al.} 2008). Views 0, 1, and 2 comprise
  382 structural MRI features (DKT regional gray-matter volumes
  \(p_1 = 160\), hierarchical cortical thickness \(p_2 = 160\), and
  native T1-weighted cortical thickness \(p_3 = 62\)). View 3 comprises
  11 neuropsychological scales with \(\ge 98\%\) completeness
  (\texttt{ADAS11}, \texttt{ADAS13}, \texttt{ADASQ4}, \texttt{MMSE},
  \texttt{CDRSB}, \texttt{TRABSCOR}, \texttt{FAQ}, and four
  \texttt{RAVLT} sub-scores), adjusted inside each training split for
  \texttt{AGE}, \texttt{PTGENDER}, and \texttt{PTEDUCAT} via
  Frisch--Waugh--Lovell projection. During training, the three MRI views
  align to View 3 via the bipartite graph
  \texttt{\{0:{[}3{]},\ 1:{[}3{]},\ 2:{[}3{]},\ 3:{[}0,1,2{]}\}}
  (\texttt{Protocol\ B}). At test time, View 3 is withheld
  (\texttt{eval\_view\_indices\ =\ {[}0,\ 1,\ 2{]}}) and models predict
  the held-out primary cognitive axis (\texttt{CogPC1} \(R^2\)) from
  structural MRI alone.
\item
  \textbf{TCGA-BRCA Multi-Omics (\texttt{MultiOmics}, \(N = 822\), 3
  views: \(p = [200, 150, 150]\)):} Primary breast invasive carcinoma
  tumors from TCGA (The Cancer Genome Atlas Network 2012) profiled
  across mRNA HiSeqV2 expression (\(p_1 = 200\)), GISTIC2 somatic
  copy-number variation (\(p_2 = 150\)), and Reverse Phase Protein Array
  (RPPA) proteomics (\(p_3 = 150\)). The downstream task is binary
  classification of clinical Estrogen Receptor (ER) status (\(76.4\%\)
  ER-positive prevalence).
\item
  \textbf{TCGA-KIRC Renal Oncology (\texttt{TCGA-KIRC}, \(N = 467\), 3
  views: \(p = [200, 150, 150]\)):} Clear cell renal cell carcinoma
  tumors from TCGA (The Cancer Genome Atlas Research Network 2013)
  profiled across mRNA (\(p_1 = 200\)), copy-number variation
  (\(p_2 = 150\)), and RPPA proteomics (\(p_3 = 150\)). The downstream
  task is binary classification of pathologic tumor stage (stage III/IV
  vs.~stage I/II).
\item
  \textbf{Cleveland Heart Disease (\texttt{Heart}, \(N = 297\), 2 views:
  \(p = [7, 6]\)):} Complete records from the UCI Cleveland Heart
  Disease cohort (Detrano et al. 1989), partitioned into resting
  clinical vitals (\(p_1 = 7\): \texttt{age}, \texttt{sex}, \texttt{cp},
  \texttt{trestbps}, \texttt{chol}, \texttt{fbs}, \texttt{restecg}) and
  exercise electrocardiography/fluoroscopy (\(p_2 = 6\):
  \texttt{thalach}, \texttt{exang}, \texttt{oldpeak}, \texttt{slope},
  \texttt{ca}, \texttt{thal}) to classify angiographic coronary artery
  disease (\texttt{num\ \textgreater{}\ 0}).
\item
  \textbf{Diabetes Progression (\texttt{Diabetes}, \(N = 442\), 2 views:
  \(p = [5, 5]\)):} The Efron et al.~cohort (Efron et al. 2004),
  partitioned into baseline anthropometrics/hemodynamics (\(p_1 = 5\):
  \texttt{age}, \texttt{sex}, \texttt{bmi}, \texttt{bp}, \texttt{s1})
  and serum lipid chemistries (\(p_2 = 5\): \texttt{s2}--\texttt{s6}) to
  predict continuous one-year disease progression (\(R^2\)).
\item
  \textbf{UCI Multiple Features (\texttt{mfeat}, \(N = 2,000\), 4 views:
  \(p = [76, 64, 6, 47]\)):} Handwritten digit descriptors across four
  feature sets (Fourier coefficients \(p_1 = 76\), Karhunen--Loève
  projections \(p_2 = 64\), morphological features \(p_3 = 6\), and
  Zernike moments \(p_4 = 47\)) for 10-class classification (\(k = 3\)).
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Synthetic Generative Regimes with Known Ground
Truth}\label{synthetic-generative-regimes-with-known-ground-truth}

To evaluate latent coordinate recovery (\(CMC\) on \(\mathbf{U}^*\)) and
sparse feature support identification (\(\text{Support } F_1\) on
\(\mathbf{V}_m^*\)) under controlled view-private noise
(\texttt{private\_scale\ =\ 0.5}), the benchmark includes six synthetic
designs spanning \(50\%\)-sparse and \(80\%\)-sparse signed and
non-negative ground-truth supports:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Clean linear multi-view with \(50\%\)-sparse signed support
  (\texttt{Linear}, \(N = 1,000, k = 3, p = [100, 80, 60]\)):}
  \(\mathbf{X}_m = \mathbf{U}^* \mathbf{V}_m^{*\top} + \mathbf{U}_{m,\text{priv}} \mathbf{V}_{m,\text{priv}}^\top + \mathbf{E}_m\)
  with shared singular scales \([1.2, 1.0, 0.85]\), private scale
  \(0.5\), and signed ground-truth loadings \(\mathbf{V}_m^*\) having
  exact \(50\%\) column sparsity (\(\text{density} = 0.50\)).
\item
  \textbf{Polynomial latent warping (\texttt{Polynomial},
  \(N = 1,000, k = 3, p = [120, 80, 50]\)):} Applies quadratic and cubic
  coordinate distortions \(\psi_m^{\text{poly}}(\mathbf{U}^*)\) in
  \(\mathbb{R}^k\) prior to linear projection \(\mathbf{V}_m^{*\top}\).
\item
  \textbf{Sinusoidal latent warping (\texttt{Sine},
  \(N = 1,000, k = 3, p = [120, 80, 50]\)):} Applies periodic
  trigonometric couplings \(\psi_m^{\text{sin}}(\mathbf{U}^*)\) prior to
  linear projection \(\mathbf{V}_m^{*\top}\).
\item
  \textbf{Asymmetric shared-plus-private variance with \(80\%\)-sparse
  signed support (\texttt{Shared+Private},
  \(N = 1,000, k = 3, p = [160, 60, 30]\)):} Shared scales decay
  (\([1.0, 0.625, 0.455]\)) while the largest view (\(p_1 = 160\))
  carries strong view-private variance
  (\(\sigma_{\text{priv},1} = 0.80\)) and each shared component loads
  onto a focal \(20\%\) signed feature support (\(80\%\) column
  sparsity).
\item
  \textbf{Non-negative disjoint parts --- dominant factor
  (\texttt{NonnegParts}, \(N = 400, k = 3, p = [120, 45, 30]\)):}
  Ground-truth bases \(\mathbf{V}_m^* \ge 0\) partition features into
  \(k = 3\) disjoint blocks (\(66.7\%\) column sparsity), and the
  outcome tracks the leading shared component \(u_1^*\).
\item
  \textbf{Non-negative disjoint parts --- weak factor with \(80\%\)
  focal sparsity (\texttt{NonnegPartsWeak},
  \(N = 400, k = 3, p = [120, 45, 30]\)):} Ground-truth bases
  \(\mathbf{V}_m^* \ge 0\) have disjoint \(20\%\)-active column supports
  (\(80\%\) column sparsity, with \(40\%\) of features in each view
  carrying pure background/private noise), and the outcome tracks the
  weakest shared component \(u_3^*\).
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Global 12-Dataset Non-Parametric Benchmark (324
Evaluations)}\label{global-12-dataset-non-parametric-benchmark-324-evaluations}

Across the \(B = 12\) dataset blocks and \(k = 9\) architectures
(\(\text{df}_1 = 8, \text{df}_2 = 88\), Nemenyi Critical Difference
\(CD = 3.468\) at \(\alpha = 0.05\)), block-averaged non-parametric
Friedman and Iman--Davenport tests reject the null hypothesis of equal
performance on both evaluation probes:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{First-layer linear probe (\(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\)):} Friedman
  \(\chi_F^2 = 29.51\) (\(p = 2.58 \times 10^{-4}\)), Iman--Davenport
  \(F_F = 4.88\) (\(p = 5.27 \times 10^{-5}\)).
\item
  \textbf{Global consensus probe (\(\mathbf{U}\)):} Friedman
  \(\chi_F^2 = 16.54\) (\(p = 0.0352\)), Iman--Davenport \(F_F = 2.29\)
  (\(p = 0.0281\)). In Table~\ref{tbl-deep-ranking-summary}, models are
  evaluated across both predictive fidelity and representation geometry.
  Optimization objectives are denoted with directional arrows: higher is
  better (\(\uparrow\)) for predictive scores, canonical mean
  correlation (\(CMC\)), and sparse support recovery (\(F_1\)), while
  lower is better (\(\downarrow\)) for Friedman ranks, scale-invariant
  Stiefel defect \(D(\mathbf{V})\), and wall-clock fit time.
\end{itemize}

The results in Table~\ref{tbl-deep-ranking-summary} and
Table~\ref{tbl-synthetic-vs-real-ranks} establish four main empirical
patterns:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Top performance on the linear OLEE basis probe (\(XV\)):}
  \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} and \texttt{NEDPP} tie for first overall on
  \(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\) (mean rank \(3.71\), mean scores
  \(0.736 \pm 0.245\) and \(0.710 \pm 0.276\)), followed closely by
  \texttt{LEND} (mean rank \(3.79\), mean score \(0.733 \pm 0.241\)) and
  \texttt{SiMLR} (mean rank \(4.25\)). Across the six real cohorts,
  \texttt{NEDPP} (mean rank \(3.75\), score \(0.665\)) and
  \texttt{SiMLR-LBFGS} (mean rank \(3.75\), score \(0.659\)) lead,
  followed by \texttt{SiMLR-LBFGSB} (mean rank \(4.08\), score
  \(0.658\)), while bound-constrained \texttt{SiMLR-LBFGSB} achieves the
  highest overall sparse support recovery
  (\(\text{Support } F_1 = 0.991\)).
\item
  \textbf{Top performance of \texttt{LEND} and \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} on
  global consensus (\(\mathbf{U}\)):} Pairing a linear OLEE encoder with
  a non-linear MLP decoder (\texttt{LEND}) achieves the best overall
  consensus rank (\(3.33\), mean \(0.696 \pm 0.266\), \(CMC = 0.775\)),
  while \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} ranks second among multi-view architectures
  (\(4.12\), mean \(0.696 \pm 0.274\), \(CMC = 0.794\)). Both non-linear
  latent decoders exceed single-matrix \texttt{PCA} (\(4.54\), score
  \(0.637 \pm 0.268\)) and pure linear solvers on consensus prediction.
\item
  \textbf{Failure of post-projection MLP encoders (\texttt{NED},
  \texttt{NEDPP}) on consensus prediction:} Inserting MLP encoder layers
  after \(\mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m\) causes overfitting on
  sample-limited cohorts: \texttt{NED} and \texttt{NEDPP} rank last on
  consensus \(\mathbf{U}\) (ranks \(6.25\) and \(7.12\), means \(0.590\)
  and \(0.573\), \(CMC = 0.589\) and \(0.652\)), even though their
  underlying first-layer bases \(\mathbf{V}_m\) remain informative when
  probed linearly (\(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\) means \(0.710\)).
\item
  \textbf{Effect-size and multiple-testing caveats:} As reported in
  Table~\ref{tbl-deep-pairwise}, on the consensus probe
  (\(\mathbf{U}\)), every linear-encoder OLEE method outperforms
  \texttt{NED} and \texttt{NEDPP} after 36-pair Holm--Bonferroni
  correction (\(p_{\text{Holm}} \le 7.5 \times 10^{-4}\)). On the
  strictly linear probe (\(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\)),
  \texttt{SiMLR-LBFGSB} outperforms \texttt{PCA} on 11 of the 12
  datasets (mean difference \(+0.0616\), raw Wilcoxon
  \(p = 9.77 \times 10^{-4}\), Holm-adjusted
  \(p_{\text{Holm}} = 0.0014\), exact permutation \(p = 0.00195\)).
  However, differences among the top cross-view OLEE methods
  (\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V}, \texttt{LEND}, \texttt{SiMLR-LBFGSB},
  \texttt{SiMLR-LBFGS}, \texttt{SiMLR}) lie within the Nemenyi critical
  difference (\(CD = 3.468\), Figure~\ref{fig-cd-diagram}) and represent
  statistical parity across the full 12-dataset aggregate.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{03_experiments_files/figure-pdf/fig-cd-diagram-output-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-cd-diagram}Linear-encoder OLEE architectures achieve
statistical parity on global consensus prediction while deep
post-projection encoders exhibit consensus degradation on sample-limited
cohorts. Demšar (2006) Critical Difference diagram across \(B = 12\)
independent dataset blocks (324 total runs, \(\alpha = 0.05\), Nemenyi
\(CD = 3.468\)) for Global Consensus Prediction (\(\mathbf{U}\)). The
horizontal axis plots mean Friedman rank from 1 to 9
(\(\leftarrow \text{Better}\), Rank 1 = Top Performer). Green markers
with asterisks (\(*\)) denote Pareto-optimal architectures; orange
markers denote dominated models. Bold horizontal bars connect cliques
differing by less than \(CD = 3.468\). Top five linear-encoder OLEE
models (\texttt{LEND} {[}3.33{]}, \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} {[}4.12{]},
\texttt{SiMLR-LBFGSB} {[}5.25{]}, \texttt{SiMLR-LBFGS} {[}5.21{]},
\texttt{SiMLR} {[}4.75{]}) form an indistinguishable equivalence clique,
while deep post-projection MLP encoders (\texttt{NED} {[}6.25{]},
\texttt{NEDPP} {[}7.12{]}) exhibit consensus degradation on
sample-limited cohorts.}

\end{figure}%

\caption{\label{fig-pareto-frontier}Multi-objective Pareto evaluation
reveals distinct efficiency frontiers balancing predictive accuracy,
fitting speed, and ground-truth support recovery across 324 runs
(\(N = 36\) runs per model point across 12 datasets \(\times\) 3 seeds).
\textbf{(A) Consensus Prediction vs.~Computational Efficiency:} Mean
Consensus Score (\(\mathbf{U}\), test \(R^2\) / accuracy, \(\uparrow\)
higher is better, range \([0.55, 0.80]\)) plotted against Mean
Wall-Clock Fit Time (seconds, \(\downarrow\) faster is better, range
\([0, 11\text{s}]\)). Dashed green line traces the speed--accuracy
Pareto frontier, spanning fast linear solvers (\texttt{SiMLR-LBFGSB} at
\(4.2\text{s}\), \texttt{SiMLR} at \(0.6\text{s}\)) to high-capacity
non-linear decoders (\texttt{LEND} at \(2.2\text{s}\),
\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} at \(8.5\text{s}\)). The top-left quadrant
represents the optimal operating envelope. \textbf{(B) Sparse Feature
Support Recovery vs.~Latent Coordinate Discovery:} Mean Sparse Support
Recovery on \(\mathbf{V}\) (Jaccard \(F_1\), \(\uparrow\) higher is
better, range \([0.28, 1.04]\)) plotted against Mean Canonical Mean
Correlation on \(\mathbf{U}\) (\(CMC\), \(\uparrow\) higher is better,
range \([0.54, 0.96]\)). The top-right quadrant represents optimal joint
recovery. \texttt{LEND} and \texttt{SiMLR-LBFGSB} define the frontier,
while unregularized \texttt{PCA} resides at the bottom-left due to
rotational sign ambiguity (\(F_1 = 0.308\)).}

\end{figure}%

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{03_experiments_files/figure-pdf/fig-three-probes-output-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-three-probes}Random Forest probing reveals that
unconstrained PCA suffers severe diagonal coordinate mixing, while
PySiMLR architectures align latent factors directly with coordinate
axes. Bar heights represent out-of-sample performance across 1,215 model
fits at operating point \(q = 0.5\) across 12 benchmark cohorts
(\(N = 135\) fits per model). Three evaluation lenses are compared:
Subspace Fidelity (rotation-invariant linear probe on
\(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\), blue bars, range \([0.48, 0.76]\)),
Single-Axis Interpretability (coordinate-sensitive Random Forest probe
on \(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\), teal bars), and Shared Patient Consensus
(\(\mathbf{U}\), amber bars). Higher is better (\(\uparrow\)) for all
three probes. Single-matrix PCA suffers a substantial axis-sensitive
drop (\(\Delta = -0.0439\)) due to diagonal coordinate mixing, whereas
PySiMLR architectures (\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V}, \texttt{LEND},
\texttt{SiMLR-LBFGSB}) preserve axis alignment, cutting the drop in half
(\(\Delta = -0.0217\text{ to }-0.0238\)) while outperforming PCA by up
to \(+65.6\%\) on patient consensus (\(\mathbf{U}\)).}

\end{figure}%

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{03_experiments_files/figure-pdf/fig-cmc-recovery-output-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-cmc-recovery}Non-linear decoding and quasi-Newton
Stiefel flow achieve superior ground-truth latent recovery across
continuous and sparse synthetic generative regimes. Grouped bars show
Canonical Mean Correlation (\(CMC\), \(\uparrow\) higher is better,
range \([0.0, 1.08]\)) between estimated consensus \(\hat{\mathbf{U}}\)
and ground-truth latent coordinates \(\mathbf{U}^*\) across 162 total
runs (6 regimes \(\times\) 9 architectures \(\times\) 3 random seeds).
Horizontal axis lists generative regimes: Linear (50\% signed sparse),
Polynomial (quadratic manifold), Sine (harmonic manifold),
Shared+Private (80\% signed sparse with view-private noise), NonnegParts
(66.7\% disjoint non-negative), and NonnegPartsWeak (80\% non-negative
with weak third factor). Colors denote architectures via \texttt{tab10}.
\texttt{LEND} leads overall (\(CMC = 0.907\)), achieving \(0.896\) on
Shared+Private (vs.~\(0.636\) for PCA) and \(0.865\) on NonnegParts
(vs.~\(0.740\) for PCA).}

\end{figure}%

Three mechanisms explain the synthetic benchmark results in
Table~\ref{tbl-synthetic-breakdown}:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Sparse support recovery requires both non-negativity and
  well-converged approximate Stiefel retraction:} On
  \texttt{NonnegParts} and \texttt{NonnegPartsWeak}, dense \texttt{PCA}
  scores at the random-partition floor (\(\text{Support } F_1 = 0.333\))
  because SVD columns are signed and rotationally unidentifiable, and
  single-matrix \texttt{NSAFlow-PCA} reaches only \(F_1 = 0.399\)
  because concatenating the views conflates view-private variance with
  the shared components. Among multi-view solvers, first-order
  \texttt{SiMLR} stops early on a gradient plateau (Stiefel frame defect
  \(D = 0.183\), sparsity \(29.3\%\)) and recovers only \(F_1 = 0.495\).
  Incorporating quasi-Newton curvature with \texttt{nsa\_flow}
  retraction in \texttt{SiMLR-LBFGS} (\(D = 1.2 \times 10^{-6}\),
  sparsity \(65.4\%\), \(F_1 = 0.966\)) and bound-constrained
  \texttt{SiMLR-LBFGSB} (\(D = 3.5 \times 10^{-9}\), sparsity
  \(66.3\%\), \(F_1 = 0.991\)) recovers the true feature supports almost
  exactly.
\item
  \textbf{Isolating weak shared signals (\texttt{NonnegPartsWeak}):}
  When the outcome depends on the third (weakest) shared factor
  \(u_3^*\), single-matrix \texttt{PCA} (\(R^2 = 0.522\) on
  \(\mathbf{U}\), \(0.518\) on \(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\)) and
  \texttt{NSAFlow-PCA} (\(0.570\) on \(\mathbf{U}\), \(0.572\) on
  \(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\)) absorb view-private variance into their
  leading axes. Cross-view alignment in \texttt{LEND} (\(0.732\) on
  \(\mathbf{U}\), \(0.741\) on \(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\)),
  \texttt{SiMLR-LBFGSB} (\(0.716\) on \(\mathbf{U}\), \(0.732\) on
  \(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\)), and \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} (\(0.703\) on
  \(\mathbf{U}\), \(0.733\) on \(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\)) preserves the
  third factor.
\item
  \textbf{Rejecting asymmetric view-private variance
  (\texttt{Shared+Private}):} When View 0 (\(p_1 = 160\)) carries large
  private variance (\(\sigma_{\text{priv},1} = 0.80\)), \texttt{PCA}
  (\(R^2 = 0.639\), \(CMC = 0.636\)) and \texttt{NSAFlow-PCA}
  (\(R^2 = 0.629\), \(CMC = 0.636\)) rotate toward View 0, whereas
  \texttt{LEND} (\(R^2 = 0.949\) on \(\mathbf{U}\), \(0.956\) on
  \(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\), \(CMC = 0.896\)) and \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V}
  (\(R^2 = 0.845\) on \(\mathbf{U}\), \(0.912\) on
  \(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\), \(CMC = 0.813\)) isolate the shared
  3-dimensional subspace.
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Clinical Neuroimaging Application: Bipartite
Cognitive Alignment in ADNI-MCI
(\(N = 1,084\))}{Clinical Neuroimaging Application: Bipartite Cognitive Alignment in ADNI-MCI (N = 1,084)}}\label{clinical-neuroimaging-application-bipartite-cognitive-alignment-in-adni-mci-n-1084}

In \texttt{ADNI-MCI}, the three structural MRI views (\(382\) regional
volume and cortical thickness features) are aligned during training
against the 11-scale cognitive battery via the bipartite graph
\texttt{\{0:{[}3{]},\ 1:{[}3{]},\ 2:{[}3{]},\ 3:{[}0,1,2{]}\}}
(\texttt{Protocol\ B}), and evaluated out-of-sample using the three MRI
views alone.

Table~\ref{tbl-adni-mci-3seed} reports the 3-seed benchmark results from
\texttt{deep\_ranking\_benchmark.csv}, and
Table~\ref{tbl-adni-mci-grid-top} reports the best configuration and
mean across valid runs for each model family in the 1,214-configuration
hyperparameter sweep (\texttt{adni\_mci\_1214\_grid\_sweep.csv}, Seed
42, \(k = 3\)).

The \texttt{ADNI-MCI} evaluations in Table~\ref{tbl-adni-mci-3seed} and
Table~\ref{tbl-adni-mci-grid-top} demonstrate three clinically relevant
findings:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Bipartite training alignment steers structural MRI bases
  toward cognitive severity:} Across three random seeds
  (Table~\ref{tbl-adni-mci-3seed}), single-matrix \texttt{PCA} and
  \texttt{NSAFlow-PCA} fitted to the 382 MRI features explain
  \(R^2 = 0.1522 \pm 0.0042\) and \(0.1590 \pm 0.0038\) of held-out
  \texttt{CogPC1} variance on the linear probe
  (\(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\)). Aligning the three MRI views against View
  3 during training via \texttt{Protocol\ B} increases held-out MRI-only
  \texttt{CogPC1} \(R^2\) to \(0.2520 \pm 0.0075\) for \texttt{LEND}
  (\(+65.6\%\) relative to \texttt{PCA}), \(0.2377 \pm 0.0149\) for
  \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} (\(+56.2\%\)), \(0.2225 \pm 0.0132\) for
  \texttt{NEDPP}, \(0.2160 \pm 0.0136\) for \texttt{NED},
  \(0.1686 \pm 0.0109\) for \texttt{SiMLR-LBFGS}, \(0.1684 \pm 0.0218\)
  for \texttt{SiMLR}, and \(0.1682 \pm 0.0124\) for
  \texttt{SiMLR-LBFGSB}.
\item
  \textbf{Consistency across optimizer families in the
  1,214-configuration sweep:} In the Seed 42 sweep
  (Table~\ref{tbl-adni-mci-grid-top}), every multi-view OLEE
  architecture family outperforms single-matrix \texttt{PCA}
  (\(0.1786\)) and \texttt{NSAFlow-PCA} (\(0.1891\)): linear Stiefel
  solvers reach \texttt{CogPC1} \(R^2 = 0.2407\)
  (\texttt{SiMLR-Lookahead}), \(0.2296\) (\texttt{SiMLR-LARS}),
  \(0.2277\) (\texttt{SiMLR-BiArmijo}), and \(0.2212\)
  (\texttt{SiMLR-LBFGSB}); deep OLEE models reach \(0.2357\)
  (\texttt{NEDPP}), \(0.2237\) (\texttt{NED}), and \(0.2190\)
  (\texttt{LEND}); and detached-prior normalizing flows reach \(0.2341\)
  (\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V}), while raising the 11-scale mean Ridge \(R^2\)
  from \(0.0813\) (\texttt{PCA}) to \(0.1054\text{–}0.1074\).
\item
  \textbf{Concatenated MRI view projections (\(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\))
  outperform 3-dimensional consensus averaging (\(\mathbf{U}\)) on
  \texttt{ADNI-MCI}:} Comparing the second and third columns of
  Table~\ref{tbl-adni-mci-3seed}, predicting \texttt{CogPC1} from the
  three view-specific MRI projections
  \([\mathbf{X}_0\mathbf{V}_0, \mathbf{X}_1\mathbf{V}_1, \mathbf{X}_2\mathbf{V}_2] \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times 9}\)
  (first-layer linear probe \(XV = 0.2520 \pm 0.0075\) for
  \texttt{LEND}, \(0.2377 \pm 0.0149\) for \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V})
  consistently exceeds predicting from their unweighted average
  \(\hat{\mathbf{U}} \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times 3}\) (consensus probe
  \(U = 0.1934 \pm 0.0116\) and \(0.2064 \pm 0.0078\)). Retaining the
  per-view OLEE projections allows the downstream linear predictor to
  weight volumetric and cortical-thickness views according to their
  distinct regional sensitivity to hippocampal and entorhinal atrophy.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Basis Geometry Ablations: Role of \(w\),
Row-Mean Control, ICA Initialization, Rank \(k\), and Anatomical Feature
Attribution}{Basis Geometry Ablations: Role of w, Row-Mean Control, ICA Initialization, Rank k, and Anatomical Feature Attribution}}\label{basis-geometry-ablations-role-of-w-row-mean-control-ica-initialization-rank-k-and-anatomical-feature-attribution}

Notice in Table~\ref{tbl-deep-ranking-summary} and
Table~\ref{tbl-adni-mci-3seed} that when the multi-view solvers are run
with unconstrained signs (\texttt{positivity="either"}, the default in
the 324-run benchmark for signed real-world z-scores), the learned bases
\(\mathbf{V}_m\) are nearly exact orthonormal frames (Stiefel defect
\(D \approx 10^{-15}\text{–}10^{-5}\)) with \(0.0\%\) exact zeros. To
clarify how the \texttt{nsa\_flow} weight \(w \in (0, 1)\),
non-negativity (\(\mathbf{V}_m \ge 0\)), per-subject row-mean control,
deterministic ICA initialization, and latent rank \(k\) jointly shape
sparsity, orthogonality, and anatomical localization, we perform four
targeted ablations on \texttt{NonnegParts} and \texttt{ADNI-MCI}
(\texttt{paper/results\_cache/ablation\_*.csv}).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Effect of the Approximate Stiefel Weight
\(w \in [0.01, 0.95]\)}{Effect of the Approximate Stiefel Weight w \textbackslash in {[}0.01, 0.95{]}}}\label{effect-of-the-approximate-stiefel-weight-w-in-0.01-0.95}

\texttt{SiMLR-LBFGSB} (\(\mathbf{V}_m \ge 0, k = 3\), deterministic ICA
initialization, \texttt{consolidate=False}) across ten values of
\(w \in [0.01, 0.95]\) on both synthetic \texttt{NonnegParts} (3-seed
mean) and real \texttt{ADNI-MCI} (bipartite \texttt{Protocol\ B} with
in-fold row-mean control).

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{03_experiments_files/figure-pdf/fig-w-sweep-output-1.pdf}}
