\caption{\label{fig-w-sweep}A convex weight of \(w = 0.50\) balances
rank preservation against combinatorial boundary stiffness across
synthetic and clinical cohorts (\(k = 3, \mathbf{V}_m \ge 0\)).
\textbf{(A) Conditioning \& Rank Collapse on ADNI-MCI (\(N = 1,084\),
age/sex/education residualized):} Left Y-axis plots normalized Stiefel
defect \(D(\mathbf{V})\) (log scale, red line, \(\downarrow\) lower is
better); right Y-axis plots Effective Rank (blue dashed line,
\(\uparrow\) higher is better, range \([1.85, 3.10]\) of \(k=3\)).
Setting \(w < 0.20\) (light red shaded rank-collapse zone) causes rank
collapse (\(\text{EffRank} = 2.02, D = 0.244\) at \(w = 0.01\));
\(w \ge 0.35\) restores full rank
(\(\text{EffRank} = 3.00, D = 6.2 \times 10^{-4}\) at \(w = 0.50\),
green dotted line). \textbf{(B) Basis Sparsity Scaling:} ADNI overall
sparsity (teal), ADNI \(\mathbf{v}_1\) sparsity (amber), and synthetic
\texttt{NonnegParts} sparsity (purple) converge to the theoretical
disjoint-support bound \(1 - 1/k = 66.7\%\) (grey dashed line) for
\(w \ge 0.35\). \textbf{(C) Wall-Clock Fit Time and Test Accuracy:} Left
Y-axis plots fit time (seconds, purple, \(\downarrow\) faster is
better); right Y-axis plots held-out ADNI \texttt{CogPC1} \(R^2\)
(green, \(\uparrow\) higher is better). Pushing \(w \to 0.95\) increases
runtime \(2.5\times\text{–}3.8\times\) due to combinatorial orthant
stiffness and lowers test \(R^2\) from \(0.1587\) to \(0.1323\).}

\end{figure}%

The sweep in Table~\ref{tbl-w-sweep} and Figure~\ref{fig-w-sweep}
explains why \(w = 0.50\) is chosen as the default weight across
\texttt{nsa\_flow} and \texttt{PySiMLR}:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Preventing rank collapse below \(w = 0.20\):} At \(w = 0.01\)
  on \texttt{ADNI-MCI}, the orthogonality penalty is too weak to counter
  the cross-view alignment gradient, which pulls the columns of
  \(\mathbf{V}_m\) together: the effective rank collapses to
  \(\text{EffRank} = 2.02\) of \(3\) (\texttt{ADNI\_Defect\_D}
  \(= 0.2442\), column overlap \(0.485\), sparsity \(22.5\%\),
  \texttt{CogPC1} \(R^2 = 0.1323\)). Raising \(w\) past the knee at
  \(w = 0.35\) restores full rank (\(\text{EffRank} = 2.99\),
  \(D = 0.0015\)) and drives sparsity to the theoretical
  \(1 - 1/3 = 66.7\%\) regime (\(63.1\%\) at \(w = 0.35\) and \(64.8\%\)
  at \(w = 0.50\)).
\item
  \textbf{Avoiding high-\(w\) boundary stiffness (\(w \ge 0.90\)):} As
  \(w\) approaches \(1\) (\(w = 0.90, 0.95\)), the fidelity weight
  \((1 - w)\) shrinks toward zero, pinning the inner L-BFGS-B retraction
  against combinatorial orthant boundaries. Wall-clock fit time
  increases \(2.5\times\) on \texttt{ADNI-MCI} (\(4.01\text{ s}\) at
  \(w = 0.50\) to \(9.85\text{ s}\) at \(w = 0.95\)) and \(3.8\times\)
  on \texttt{NonnegParts} (\(2.36\text{ s}\) to \(8.87\text{ s}\)),
  while \texttt{ADNI-MCI} test \(R^2\) drops from \(0.1587\)
  (\(w = 0.50\)) to \(0.1323\) (\(w = 0.95\)).
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Controlling for Row Means, Deterministic ICA
Initialization, and the \(1 - 1/k\) Sparsity
Law}{Controlling for Row Means, Deterministic ICA Initialization, and the 1 - 1/k Sparsity Law}}\label{controlling-for-row-means-deterministic-ica-initialization-and-the-1---1k-sparsity-law}

To test why Component 1 (\(\mathbf{v}_1\)) is much denser than
Components \(2, \dots, k\) under standard column-centering and PCA
initialization---and whether row-mean removal and deterministic ICA
initialization resolve this
asymmetry---Table~\ref{tbl-rowmean-ica-ablation} compares all
combinations of preprocessing (\texttt{Col-Centered}
vs.~\texttt{Row+Col\ Centered}), initialization (\texttt{PCA}
vs.~deterministic \texttt{ICA}), and positivity (\texttt{Signed}
vs.~\texttt{Non-Negative}) on \texttt{ADNI-MCI} (\(k = 3, w = 0.50\)).
Table~\ref{tbl-k-sparsity-sweep} and Figure~\ref{fig-rowmean-k-ablation}
then track how overall sparsity and \(\mathbf{v}_1\) sparsity scale
across latent ranks \(k \in \{2, 3, 5, 8, 12\}\).

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{03_experiments_files/figure-pdf/fig-rowmean-k-ablation-output-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-rowmean-k-ablation}Removing per-subject row means
with deterministic ICA initialization prevents Component 1
(\(\mathbf{v}_1\)) from collapsing onto the diffuse all-ones brain-size
vector on \texttt{ADNI-MCI} (\(N = 1,084\),
\(w = 0.50, \mathbf{V}_m \ge 0\), in-fold age/sex/education
residualization). \textbf{(A) Sparsity Distribution across Components
(\(k = 3\)):} Grouped bars plot Component 1 (\(\mathbf{v}_1\)) sparsity
(red bars, \(\uparrow\)), Components 2 and 3 (\(\mathbf{v}_{2,3}\))
sparsity (blue bars, \(\uparrow\)), and overall basis sparsity (teal
bars, \(\uparrow\)). Under standard column-centering with PCA
initialization, \(\mathbf{v}_1\) remains \(35.8\%\) sparse while
\(\mathbf{v}_{2,3}\) reach \(76.6\%\). Row-mean centering with
deterministic ICA initialization equalizes sparsity across all
components (\(\mathbf{v}_1 = 69.4\%, \mathbf{v}_{2,3} = 65.9\%\)),
matching the theoretical disjoint bound \(1 - 1/3 = 66.7\%\) (grey
dashed line). \textbf{(B) Sparsity Scaling Across Ranks
\(k \in \{2, 3, 5, 8, 12\}\):} Under standard column-centering with PCA,
\(\mathbf{v}_{2\dots k}\) tracks the theoretical \(1 - 1/k\) curve (blue
squares, \(62.9\% \to 81.9\%\)) while \(\mathbf{v}_1\) becomes
progressively denser (red circles, \(48.1\% \to 24.5\%\)). Row-mean
control with ICA initialization (green triangles) keeps \(\mathbf{v}_1\)
sparse (\(67.1\% \to 53.4\%\)), preventing mean-vector collapse.}

\end{figure}%

Table~\ref{tbl-rowmean-ica-ablation} and
Table~\ref{tbl-k-sparsity-sweep} establish four mechanistic conclusions:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Positivity (\(\mathbf{V}_m \ge 0\)) is the switch that
  activates Stiefel sparsity:} In every \texttt{Signed\ (Unconstrained)}
  row of Table~\ref{tbl-rowmean-ica-ablation}, exact zero sparsity is
  \(0.0\%\) (\texttt{sparsity\_all} \(= 1.3\%\text{–}3.3\%\) at the
  \(1\%\) relative threshold) even though \texttt{defect\_D}
  \(\le 4.5 \times 10^{-5}\). Imposing
  \texttt{Non-Negative\ (V\textgreater{}=0)} immediately raises overall
  sparsity to \(57.8\%\text{–}67.1\%\) (\(57.4\%\text{–}65.9\%\) exact
  zeros) at \(k = 3\).
\item
  \textbf{Why \(\mathbf{v}_1\) is dense under standard column-centering
  and PCA initialization:} Under
  \texttt{Col-Centered\ +\ PCA\ +\ Non-Neg}, Component 1 has only
  \(35.8\%\) sparsity (\(33.8\%\) exact zeros, Hoyer sparseness
  \(0.339\)), whereas Components 2 and 3 have \(76.6\%\) sparsity.
  Moreover, as \(k\) increases from \(2\) to \(12\)
  (Table~\ref{tbl-k-sparsity-sweep}), \texttt{Col-Centered\ +\ PCA}
  drives \(\mathbf{v}_{2\dots k}\) sparsity from \(62.9\%\) to
  \(81.9\%\) (tracking \(1 - 1/k\)), but \textbf{\(\mathbf{v}_1\)
  sparsity drops from \(48.1\%\) (\(k = 2\)) to \(24.5\%\)
  (\(k = 12\))}. Because \(\mathbf{v}_1\) catches the positive global
  row mean across parcels, it remains a dense global size vector
  regardless of \(k\).
\item
  \textbf{Why ICA initialization requires row-mean removal to stay
  sparse during multi-view fitting:} Under
  \texttt{Col-Centered\ +\ ICA\ +\ Non-Neg}, deterministic ICA
  initialization starts with a sparse Component 1 at \texttt{Init-Only}
  (\(\mathbf{v}_1\) sparsity \(72.3\%\), exact zeros \(70.1\%\)).
  However, once \texttt{Bipartite\ SiMR} optimization runs on uncentered
  rows, the reconstruction term pulls \(\mathbf{v}_1\) back onto the
  global subject mean, reducing \(\mathbf{v}_1\) sparsity from
  \(72.3\%\) to \(39.3\%\).
\item
  \textbf{Row-mean control + deterministic ICA initialization restores
  uniform sparsity across all components:} When per-subject row means
  are removed inside the training fold (\texttt{Row+Col\ Centered}),
  \texttt{PCA} initialization raises fitted \(\mathbf{v}_1\) sparsity
  from \(35.8\%\) to \(52.9\%\) (with \texttt{CogPC1} \(R^2 = 0.1776\)
  when the scalar row mean is retained alongside
  \(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\), exceeding standard \texttt{PCA} \(0.1628\)).
  Combining \texttt{Row+Col\ Centered} with deterministic \texttt{ICA}
  initialization raises fitted \(\mathbf{v}_1\) sparsity to
  \textbf{\(69.4\%\) (\(67.5\%\) exact zeros, Hoyer \(0.701\))} and
  \(\mathbf{v}_{2,3}\) sparsity to \textbf{\(65.9\%\)} (\(67.1\%\)
  overall, \(D = 5.8 \times 10^{-5}\)), achieving uniform
  \(1 - 1/3 = 66.7\%\) sparsity across every column.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Quantile \texttt{sparseness} Ablation
(\texttt{default=0.5}, Operating Points \texttt{0.5} and \texttt{0.8})
on ADNI-MCI and
NonnegParts}{Quantile sparseness Ablation (default=0.5, Operating Points 0.5 and 0.8) on ADNI-MCI and NonnegParts}}\label{quantile-sparseness-ablation-default0.5-operating-points-0.5-and-0.8-on-adni-mci-and-nonnegparts}

While row-mean removal with deterministic ICA initialization equalizes
sparsity across columns at the \(1 - 1/k = 66.7\%\) bound, subtracting
each participant's global cortical row mean prior to optimization lowers
raw \texttt{CogPC1} \(R^2\) from \(0.1892\)
(\texttt{Col-Centered\ +\ PCA\ +\ Non-Neg}) to \(0.1408\) (\(0.1531\)
when the scalar row mean is re-appended). Moreover, domain investigators
frequently require a specific per-column sparsity level (e.g., the two
canonical operating points \(q = 0.50\) and \(q = 0.80\) from Avants et
al. (2021)) without inflating \(k\).

Table~\ref{tbl-quantile-sparseness} evaluates the R-style quantile
\texttt{sparseness} parameter (\texttt{sparseness}
\(\in \{\text{None}, 0.50, 0.70, 0.80, 0.85, 0.90\}\), applied to the
three structural MRI views as \texttt{{[}q,\ q,\ q,\ None{]}}, with
universal default \texttt{DEFAULT\_SPARSENESS\ =\ 0.5} across all
linear, deep, flow, and \texttt{sklearn} algorithms in \texttt{PySiMLR})
under standard column-centering (\texttt{Col-Centered\ +\ PCA\ Init},
\(k = 3, w = 0.50\)) for both \texttt{positivity="positive"}
(\(\mathbf{V}_m \ge 0\)) and \texttt{positivity="either"}.

Table~\ref{tbl-quantile-sparseness} demonstrates three practical
properties of the quantile \texttt{sparseness} operator:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{The two canonical operating points (\(q = 0.50\) default and
  \(q = 0.80\) high sparsity) and fine-grained control up to
  \(q = 0.85\):} Following Avants et al. (2021), \texttt{PySiMLR} sets
  \texttt{sparseness\ =\ 0.50} as the universal default across all
  linear (\texttt{simlr}, \texttt{SiMLR-LBFGS}, \texttt{SiMLR-LBFGSB}),
  deep (\texttt{lend\_simr}, \texttt{ned\_simr},
  \texttt{ned\_simr\_shared\_private}), flow (\texttt{flow\_simr\_v}),
  and \texttt{sklearn} estimators. At the default
  \texttt{sparseness\ =\ 0.50}, Component 1 (\(\mathbf{v}_1\)) sparsity
  rises from \(35.8\%\) (\(55/62\) active DKT regions at
  \texttt{sparseness=None}) to \(61.5\%\) (\(31/62\) active DKT regions,
  \(74.5\%\) overall sparsity, \texttt{Synth\ F1} \(= 0.915\)) for
  \(\mathbf{V}_m \ge 0\), and to \(55.7\%\) (\(31/62\) active DKT
  regions, \texttt{CogPC1} \(R^2 = 0.1968\)) for signed bases. At the
  high-sparsity canonical operating point \texttt{sparseness\ =\ 0.80},
  \(\mathbf{v}_1\) sparsity reaches \(81.9\%\) (\(85.8\%\) overall,
  \(12/62\) active DKT regions on \(\mathbf{v}_1\)) for
  \(\mathbf{V}_m \ge 0\) and \(84.2\%\) (\(85.0\%\) overall,
  \((12, 12, 12)/62\) active DKT regions) for signed bases. Pushing to
  \texttt{sparseness\ =\ 0.85} under \(\mathbf{V}_m \ge 0\) achieves
  \textbf{\(85.5\%\) \(\mathbf{v}_1\) sparsity (\(88.0\%\) overall)}
  with an exactly balanced \((9, 9, 9)/62\) active-region count across
  all three components and \texttt{CogPC1} \(R^2 = 0.1923\)
  (\texttt{11-scale} \(R^2 = 0.0897\))---exceeding both
  \texttt{sparseness=None} (\(0.1892\)) and dense \texttt{PCA}
  (\(0.1904\)).
\item
  \textbf{Enabling sparse support recovery and accuracy gains even for
  signed bases (\texttt{positivity="either"}):} When
  \texttt{positivity="either"} and \texttt{sparseness=None}, orthogonal
  columns have \(0\%\) exact zeros
  (\texttt{Active\ DKT\ =\ (62,\ 62,\ 62)/62},
  \texttt{Synth\ F1\ =\ 0.333}, \texttt{CogPC1} \(R^2 = 0.1714\)). The
  default \texttt{sparseness\ =\ 0.50} immediately enforces
  \((31, 31, 31)/62\) active DKT regions and raises \texttt{CogPC1}
  \(R^2\) to \(0.1968\) (\texttt{Synth\ F1\ =\ 0.621}), while
  \texttt{sparseness\ =\ 0.70} enforces \((19, 19, 19)/62\) active DKT
  regions (\(75.7\%\) overall sparsity,
  \(D(\mathbf{V}) = 2.00 \times 10^{-6}\)), raises synthetic
  \texttt{NonnegParts} support recovery to \(0.791\), and achieves
  \textbf{\texttt{CogPC1} \(R^2 = 0.2089\)} (\texttt{11-scale}
  \(R^2 = 0.0943\)).
\item
  \textbf{Choosing between \texttt{sparseness\ =\ 0.5} (default),
  \texttt{0.8} (high sparsity), and \texttt{None} (un-thresholded
  \texttt{nsa\_flow}):} At \(k \ge 3\), the default \(q = 0.50\) is
  milder than the non-negative Stiefel disjoint-support bound
  \(1 - 1/k \ge 66.7\%\); thus, on synthetic block-partitioned data
  (\texttt{NonnegParts}, \(k = 3\)), \(q = 0.50\) preserves high support
  recovery (\(F_1 = 0.915\), vs.~\(0.941\) at \texttt{sparseness=None})
  while stripping the diffuse global-mean floor off \(\mathbf{v}_1\) on
  real neuroimaging cohorts. When \(p_m\) is large and focal biomarker
  panels are desired, \(q = 0.80\text{--}0.85\) isolates compact
  top-\(15\%\text{--}20\%\) anatomical or genomic signatures, whereas
  \texttt{sparseness=None} remains available whenever pure
  un-thresholded \texttt{nsa\_flow} retraction is desired.
\end{enumerate}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{03_experiments_files/figure-pdf/fig-adni-anatomical-heatmap-output-1.pdf}}

\caption{\label{fig-adni-anatomical-heatmap}Quantile-sparsified
non-negative SiMR (\(q = 0.85\)) isolates localized Braak-staged
Alzheimer's disease cortical networks while maintaining peak predictive
accuracy on ADNI-MCI (\(N = 1,084\), age/sex/education residualized,
\(k = 3, w = 0.50\)). Heatmaps display basis weights \(\mathbf{V}_2\)
across all 62 Desikan-Killiany-Tourville (DKT) cortical thickness
parcels (rows ordered by dominant component assignment in Panel D).
\textbf{(A) Dense PCA (\texttt{CogPC1} \(R^2 = 0.1904\)):} Evaluated
with diverging colormap \texttt{RdBu\_r} (scale \([-0.45, +0.45]\)).
Component 1 assigns uniform negative weight (\(\approx -0.15\)) across
all 62 parcels (\(0.0\%\) sparsity). \textbf{(B) Standard Non-Negative
SiMR (\texttt{sparseness=None}, \(R^2 = 0.1892\)):} Evaluated with
sequential colormap \texttt{YlGnBu} (scale \([0.0, 0.65]\)). Component 1
remains \(85.5\%\) dense (\(14.5\%\) sparsity) to capture global
cortical thickness. \textbf{(C) Row-Centered + ICA-Initialized SiMR
(\texttt{sparseness=None}, \(R^2 = 0.1531\)):} All three components are
uniformly sparse (\(75.8\%, 69.4\%, 67.7\%\)) at reduced predictive
\(R^2\). \textbf{(D) Quantile-Sparsified SiMR (\(q = 0.85\),
\(R^2 = 0.1923\)):} Enforces exact \((9, 9, 9)/62\) active parcels per
component (\(85.5\%, 95.2\%, 91.9\%\) sparsity at the \(1\%\) threshold)
while achieving the highest non-negative predictive accuracy: Component
1 isolates the lateral temporal-temporoparietal association axis (Braak
IV--V); Component 2 isolates the medial temporal amnestic core (Braak
I--III); Component 3 isolates the frontocingulate salience network; and
primary somatomotor cortex (\texttt{right\_precentral} \(= 0.0016\))
serves as a physiologically preserved negative control.}

\end{figure}%

Comparing the four panels in Table~\ref{tbl-adni-anatomical-loadings}
and Figure~\ref{fig-adni-anatomical-heatmap} reveals the concrete
neuroanatomical difference between the bases:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Dense PCA (Figure~\ref{fig-adni-anatomical-heatmap} A):}
  Component 1 assigns nearly identical negative weight
  (\(\approx -0.146\) to \(-0.150\)) across all 62 cortical regions
  (\(0.0\%\) sparsity), representing a non-specific global cortical
  average, while Components 2 and 3 mix positive occipital loadings with
  negative frontal loadings across the entire cortex.
\item
  \textbf{Standard Non-Negative SiMR with \texttt{sparseness=None}
  (Figure~\ref{fig-adni-anatomical-heatmap} B):} Without quantile
  thresholding or row-mean removal, Component 1 remains \(85.5\%\) dense
  on View 2 (\(14.5\%\) sparsity, \(55/62\) active regions), spreading
  almost uniform weight (\(+0.204\) to \(+0.229\)) across temporal,
  parietal, and frontal parcels to capture each participant's global
  cortical thickness. Because \texttt{nsa\_flow} enforces approximate
  orthogonality (\(D = 8.2 \times 10^{-5}\)) with
  \(\mathbf{V}_m \ge 0\), Components 2 and 3 are squeezed into the
  remaining parcels (\(90.3\%\) and \(80.6\%\) sparsity).
\item
  \textbf{Row-Centered + ICA-Initialized Non-Negative SiMR
  (Figure~\ref{fig-adni-anatomical-heatmap} C):} Removing the subject
  row mean and initializing with deterministic ICA produces three
  uniformly sparse networks (\(75.8\%, 69.4\%,\) and \(67.7\%\)
  sparsity), isolating occipital/sensorimotor (\(\mathbf{v}_1\)),
  orbitofrontal/cingulate (\(\mathbf{v}_2\)), and bilateral
  entorhinal/parahippocampal (\(\mathbf{v}_3\)) subnetworks, but lowers
  \texttt{CogPC1} \(R^2\) to \(0.1408\) (\(0.1531\) with row mean).
\item
  \textbf{Quantile-Sparsified Non-Negative SiMR
  (\texttt{sparseness\ =\ 0.85},
  Figure~\ref{fig-adni-anatomical-heatmap} D):} Retaining standard
  column-centering and setting \texttt{sparseness\ =\ 0.85} restricts
  every column to \textbf{exact \((9, 9, 9)/62\) active DKT regions}
  (\(85.5\%, 95.2\%,\) and \(91.9\%\) sparsity at the \(1\%\) threshold)
  while achieving the highest non-negative \texttt{CogPC1}
  \(R^2 = 0.1923\), isolating three established neuropathological stages
  of Alzheimer's disease:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    \textbf{Component 1 (\(\mathbf{v}_1\), \(85.5\%\) sparse, \(9/62\)
    active): Lateral Temporal--Temporoparietal Association Axis (Braak
    Stages IV--V):} Strongly loads \texttt{left\_middle\_temporal}
    (\(+0.670\)), \texttt{left\_supramarginal} (\(+0.454\)),
    \texttt{right\_middle\_temporal} (\(+0.341\)),
    \texttt{right\_pars\_opercularis} (\(+0.319\)), and
    \texttt{left\_superior\_frontal} (\(+0.209\)). In clinical
    Alzheimer's disease, temporoparietal neocortical atrophy tracks the
    clinical transition from mild memory lapses to overt semantic
    impairment, anomia, and executive slowing.
  \item
    \textbf{Component 2 (\(\mathbf{v}_2\), \(95.2\%\) sparse, \(9/62\)
    active): Medial Temporal Amnestic Core (Braak Stages I--III):}
    Isolates the pathognomonic neurodegenerative epicenter of early
    Alzheimer's disease, led by \texttt{left\_entorhinal} (\(+0.680\)),
    \texttt{right\_entorhinal} (\(+0.226\)), and posterior
    precuneal/visual hub \texttt{right\_cuneus} (\(+0.698\)), aligned
    with subcortical hippocampal atrophy in View 0
    (\texttt{right\_hippocampus} \(+0.369\)). This component captures
    the transentorhinal and entorhinal-hippocampal disconnection
    directly responsible for episodic memory encoding failure on the Rey
    Auditory Verbal Learning Test (\texttt{RAVLT}).
  \item
    \textbf{Component 3 (\(\mathbf{v}_3\), \(91.9\%\) sparse, \(9/62\)
    active): Frontocingulate Salience Network:} Loads
    \texttt{left\_rostral\_middle\_frontal} (\(+0.631\)),
    \texttt{right\_caudal\_anterior\_cingulate} (\(+0.478\)),
    \texttt{left\_insula} (\(+0.434\)), and \texttt{right\_insula}
    (\(+0.429\)). This fronto-insular-cingulate network governs
    motivation, behavioral regulation, and salience detection; its
    disruption underlies apathy and executive set-shifting deficits
    (measured by \texttt{TRABSCOR}) in progressing MCI.
  \item
    \textbf{Biological Sparing as a Negative Control:} Primary
    somatomotor cortex (\texttt{right\_precentral} \(+0.0016\),
    \texttt{left\_precentral} \(\le 10^{-5}\)) and primary somatosensory
    cortex (\texttt{left\_postcentral} \(\le 10^{-5}\)) receive
    negligible loadings across all three components. This mirrors the
    clinicopathological hallmark of Alzheimer's disease---the
    near-complete preservation of primary motor and sensory neocortex
    until end-stage dementia---demonstrating that non-negative sparse
    regularization isolates genuine disease topology rather than
    arbitrary geometric partitions.
  \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

Comparing the six real cohorts in Table~\ref{tbl-real-cohort-breakdown}
clarifies when multi-view alignment provides a measurable advantage over
single-matrix \texttt{PCA} and \texttt{NSAFlow-PCA}:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Four-view heterogeneous cohorts (\texttt{ADNI-MCI} and
  \texttt{mfeat}):} When views differ substantially in dimensionality
  and noise structure (\(p = [160, 160, 62, 11]\) in \texttt{ADNI-MCI};
  \(p = [76, 64, 6, 47]\) in \texttt{mfeat}), single-matrix \texttt{PCA}
  is dominated by the largest views (\texttt{mfeat}
  \(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\) accuracy \(0.727 \pm 0.006\), \(\mathbf{U}\)
  accuracy \(0.732 \pm 0.004\)). Multi-view OLEE alignment equalizes
  view contributions, raising \texttt{mfeat} \(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\)
  accuracy to \(0.967 \pm 0.009\) (\texttt{NEDPP}), \(0.964 \pm 0.009\)
  (\texttt{NED}), and \(0.943 \pm 0.008\) (\texttt{SiMLR-LBFGSB}), and
  raising 3-dimensional consensus \(\mathbf{U}\) accuracy to
  \(0.793 \pm 0.013\) (\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V}) and \(0.785 \pm 0.017\)
  (\texttt{LEND}).
\item
  \textbf{Three-view cancer genomics (\texttt{MultiOmics}
  {[}TCGA-BRCA{]} and \texttt{TCGA-KIRC}):} In oncology, phenotypic
  progression reflects coordinated information flow across the Central
  Dogma---from somatic DNA copy-number alterations (CNV) to mRNA
  expression and functional proteomic phosphorylation.

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    On \texttt{MultiOmics} (TCGA-BRCA ER status, \(N = 822\)),
    multi-view models (\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} \(0.927 \pm 0.007\),
    \texttt{LEND} \(0.927 \pm 0.007\), \texttt{NEDPP}
    \(0.927 \pm 0.007\)) and linear solvers (\texttt{SiMLR-LBFGSB}
    \(0.921 \pm 0.018\)) outperform single-matrix \texttt{PCA}
    (\(0.915 \pm 0.018\)). The linear dictionary \(\mathbf{V}_m\)
    isolates the core hormonal oncogenic circuit across modalities:
    estrogen receptor alpha protein (\texttt{ER-alpha}), master
    transcription factor \texttt{GATA3}, activating phosphorylation site
    \texttt{ER-alpha\_pS118}, downstream luminal transcripts
    (\texttt{PGR}, \texttt{PIP}), and focal amplicons at 8p11-p12 and
    17q23. Quantile sparsity eliminates hundreds of passenger
    copy-number alterations, isolating the driver pathway.
  \item
    On \texttt{TCGA-KIRC} (clear cell renal cell carcinoma pathologic
    stage classification, \(N = 467\)), non-linear decoders downstream
    of OLEE demonstrate distinct utility: \texttt{LEND}
    (\(0.714 \pm 0.004\)), \texttt{NED} (\(0.697 \pm 0.023\)), and
    \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} (\(0.688 \pm 0.007\)) achieve superior
    consensus \(\mathbf{U}\) accuracy over linear \texttt{PCA}
    (\(0.671 \pm 0.015\)), while \texttt{SiMLR-LBFGS} leads the linear
    probe \(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\) (\(0.688 \pm 0.017\)). In renal
    oncology, advanced tumor stage (Stage III/IV vs.~I/II) is marked by
    metabolic reprogramming, von Hippel-Lindau (\texttt{VHL})
    loss-driven pseudo-hypoxia, and mTOR/PI3K signaling. Non-linear
    normalizing flows and decoders capture these non-linear cross-assay
    interactions while maintaining feature-level transparency in the
    linear dictionary \(\mathbf{V}_m\).
  \end{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Low-dimensional two-view clinical tabular cohorts
  (\texttt{Heart} and \texttt{Diabetes}):} On \texttt{Heart}
  (\(p = [7, 6]\)), \texttt{NSAFlow-PCA} achieves the highest linear
  probe accuracy (\(0.848 \pm 0.006\) in \(0.004\text{ s}\) with
  \(32.1\%\) sparsity) and ties \texttt{PCA} on consensus accuracy
  (\(0.837 \pm 0.006\)). On \texttt{Diabetes} (\(p = [5, 5]\)),
  \texttt{NSAFlow-PCA} leads consensus prediction
  (\(R^2 = 0.382 \pm 0.051\)), whereas \texttt{SiMLR-LBFGSB}
  (\(0.396 \pm 0.110\)) and \texttt{SiMLR-LBFGS} (\(0.396 \pm 0.111\))
  lead the concatenated linear probe \(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\)
  (vs.~\texttt{PCA} \(0.351 \pm 0.048\)).
\end{itemize}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Multi-Operating-Point Sparseness
(\(q \in \{\text{None}, 0.5, 0.8\}\)) and Positivity Evaluation (1,215
Fits)}{Multi-Operating-Point Sparseness (q \textbackslash in \textbackslash\{\textbackslash text\{None\}, 0.5, 0.8\textbackslash\}) and Positivity Evaluation (1,215 Fits)}}\label{multi-operating-point-sparseness-q-in-textnone-0.5-0.8-and-positivity-evaluation-1215-fits}

To evaluate how the 3-stage support-masked
\texttt{nsa\_flow(sparseness=q)} polish interacts with the underlying
ground-truth density and sign constraints across the full benchmark
suite, we execute \(1,215\) model fits
(\texttt{paper/results\_cache/operating\_points\_05\_08\_benchmark.csv})
spanning three canonical operating points---unsparsified
(\(q = \text{None}\)), moderate feature selection (\(q = 0.5\), \(50\%\)
zeros), and focal biomarker selection (\(q = 0.8\), \(80\%\) zeros)---as
well as paired \texttt{positivity="either"}
vs.~\texttt{positivity="positive"} evaluations on the high-dimensional
real biomedical cohorts (\texttt{ADNI-MCI}, \texttt{MultiOmics},
\texttt{TCGA-KIRC}).

exploratory discovery (\(q = 0.5\)) and compact biomarker isolation
(\(q = 0.8\)) across 1,215 model fits in 12 cohorts. \textbf{(A)
Subspace \& Axis Fidelity vs.~Basis Sparsity:} Left Y-axis plots

\([0.63, 0.69]\)); right Y-axis plots achieved basis sparsity \%
(\(\uparrow\) more compact, green dash-dot line, range
\([10\%, 75\%]\)). Moderate thresholding (\(q = 0.5\)) achieves peak
linear fidelity (\(0.6793\), solid blue) and peak Random Forest axis
interpretability (\(0.6505\), dashed violet), outperforming unsparsified
models (\(q = \text{None}\)). Focal selection (\(q = 0.8\)) preserves
\(99.1\%\) of linear fidelity while raising sparsity to \(61.9\%\).
\textbf{(B) Sparse Ground-Truth Support Recovery (\(F_1\)):} Y-axis
plots Jaccard \(F_1\) (\(\uparrow\) higher is better, range
\([0.15, 0.85]\)). On 50\%-sparse ground truth (\texttt{Linear}, blue
circles), \(q = 0.5\) peaks at \(F_1 = 0.727\) (vs.~\(0.534\) at
\(q = \text{None}\)). On 80\%-sparse ground truth
(\texttt{NonnegPartsWeak}, amber triangles), \(q = 0.8\) peaks at
\(F_1 = 0.710\) (vs.~\(0.482\) at \(q = \text{None}\)). Operating points
align with intrinsic feature densities.}

The operating points and axis-sensitivity evaluations in
Table~\ref{tbl-operating-points-axis-linear} and
Figure~\ref{fig-operating-points-tradeoff} establish three practical
conclusions:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Random Forest reveals rotational diagonal mixing:} Ordinary
  least squares linear regression on \(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\) is
  rotationally invariant under orthogonal basis changes
  (\(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\mathbf{R}\) spans the same subspace for any
  \(\mathbf{R} \in \mathrm{O}(k)\)), which conceals coordinate-mixing
  defects. Decision trees and Random Forests evaluate axis-orthogonal
  splits (\(z_j \le \theta\)). When true biological factors are
  diagonally mixed across coordinates---as in unconstrained PCA---Random
  Forest prediction drops significantly. On \texttt{NonnegPartsWeak},
  PCA linear score is \(0.1246\), but its Random Forest score collapses
  to \(-0.000053\) (effectively \(0.0\%\)), and on \texttt{ADNI-MCI},
  PCA drops from \(0.1522\) to \(0.0441\) (\(>70\%\) drop). In contrast,
  \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} (\(0.2311 \to 0.1969\) on ADNI-MCI) and
  \texttt{LEND} (\(0.5140 \to 0.3855\) on \texttt{NonnegPartsWeak})
  maintain single-axis alignment, cutting the average axis drop in half
  relative to PCA across all 12 benchmark cohorts (\(-0.0226\)
  vs.~\(-0.0439\)).
\item
  \textbf{Tunable operating points match empirical feature densities:}
  Selecting \(q = 0.5\) provides a robust operating point for broad
  exploratory discovery, maximizing linear fidelity (\(0.6793\)) and
  Random Forest axis interpretability (\(0.6505\)) while recovering
  \(F_1 = 0.727\) on 50\%-sparse ground truth (\texttt{Linear}). For
  targeted diagnostic biomarker panels where physical assay footprint
  must be minimized, \(q = 0.8\) isolates focal feature sets (61.9\%
  mean sparsity) while recovering \(F_1 = 0.710\) on 80\%-sparse ground
  truth (\texttt{NonnegPartsWeak}) and \(F_1 = 0.797\) on
  \texttt{Shared+Private}, retaining \(99.1\%\) of unsparsified subspace
  predictive power.
\item
  \textbf{Paired positivity resolves reflection gauge degeneracies:}
  Enforcing non-negativity (\(\mathbf{V}_m \ge 0\)) with per-column
  sign-gauge resolution restricts basis columns to the positive orthant.
  On disjoint synthetic benchmarks (\texttt{NonnegParts}), non-negative
  constraints raise ground-truth support recovery to \(F_1 = 0.861\)
  (\texttt{SiMLR-LBFGSB}) and \(0.811\) (\texttt{LEND}), eliminating
  sign indeterminacies and establishing coordinate identifiability.
\end{enumerate}

\bookmarksetup{startatroot}

\chapter{Discussion}\label{discussion}

\textbf{SiMR} (\emph{Similarity-driven Multi-view Representation})
generalizes classical Similarity-driven Multi-view Linear Reconstruction
(\textbf{SiMLR}) (Avants et al. 2021) around a single architectural
invariant: every view \(m\) enters through a linear loading matrix
\(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}} = \mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m\) regularized by
Non-negative Stiefel Approximating Flow (\texttt{nsa\_flow}) ({Avants et
al.} 2025), while downstream consensus alignment, decoding
(\texttt{LEND}), or density estimation (\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V}) may be
linear or non-linear. Across 324 multi-seed evaluations on 12 datasets,
a 1,214-configuration sweep on \texttt{ADNI-MCI} (\(N = 1,084\)), and
1,215 operating-point fits, this formulation preserves feature-level
attribution in native measurement units while matching or exceeding
unconstrained baselines depending on data geometry.

\section{When Multi-View SiMR Models Outperform Single-Matrix PCA---and
Why PCA Often
Suffices}\label{when-multi-view-simr-models-outperform-single-matrix-pcaand-why-pca-often-suffices}

A central practical question for clinical and translational
investigators is when to use multi-view alignment versus single-matrix
\texttt{PCA} or \texttt{NSAFlow-PCA}, and why linear probes often fail
to separate them on simple cohorts. Two mechanisms govern this behavior:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Subspace rotation invariance of linear regression probes:} Any
  invertible transformation
  \(\mathbf{M} \in \mathrm{GL}(k, \mathbb{R})\) leaves
  \(\operatorname{span}(\mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m \mathbf{M}) = \operatorname{span}(\mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m)\)
  unchanged. When a dataset has few features (\(p \le 15\) in
  \texttt{Heart} and \texttt{Diabetes}) or when the shared disease
  signal already dominates the top principal components
  (\texttt{MultiOmics} ER status), \texttt{PCA} and \texttt{SiMR} span
  nearly the same \(k\)-dimensional subspace and therefore achieve
  similar linear prediction scores---even though \texttt{PCA} mixes all
  features densely while \texttt{SiMR} (\(\mathbf{V}_m \ge 0\))
  localizes them onto sparse, disjoint pathways.
\item
  \textbf{Rejection of view-private variance and bipartite supervision:}
  Multi-view SiMR outperforms \texttt{PCA} on predictive accuracy when
  individual views carry large view-private noise
  (\texttt{Shared+Private}, \texttt{NonnegPartsWeak}), when views differ
  sharply in dimensionality (\texttt{mfeat}), or when training aligns
  predictor views against a supervisory clinical battery withheld at
  deployment (\texttt{ADNI-MCI}).
\end{enumerate}

Table~\ref{tbl-method-selection} summarizes our empirical
recommendations by data regime.

cortical thickness \& volumes,
\(\mathbf{V}_m \ge 0, \text{sparseness=None}\)) & \texttt{SiMLR-LBFGSB}
(\(w = 0.5\)) with in-fold row-mean removal + deterministic \texttt{ICA}
initialization & Prevents \(\mathbf{v}_1\) from catching the global
brain-size mean, raising \(\mathbf{v}_1\) sparsity from \(35.8\%\) to
\(69.4\%\) (\(67.1\%\) overall, \(D = 5.8 \times 10^{-5}\)) and
isolating bilateral entorhinal/parahippocampal cortex \\
\textbf{Sparse non-negative support discovery} (\texttt{NonnegParts},
\texttt{NonnegPartsWeak}) & \texttt{SiMLR-LBFGSB} or
\texttt{SiMLR-LBFGS} (\(w = 0.5, \mathbf{V}_m \ge 0\),
\texttt{sparseness=0.5} or \texttt{None}) & Recovers ground-truth sparse
supports with \(F_1 = 0.991\) and \(0.966\) (vs.~\texttt{PCA} \(0.333\)
and first-order \texttt{SiMLR} \(0.495\)) \\
\textbf{Curved manifolds or strong view-private noise}
(\texttt{Shared+Private}, \texttt{Polynomial}, \texttt{TCGA-KIRC},
\texttt{mfeat} consensus) & \texttt{LEND} or \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} &
\texttt{LEND} ranks \#1 on consensus \(\mathbf{U}\) (\(3.33\),
\(CMC = 0.775\), \texttt{Shared+Private} \(R^2 = 0.949\)
vs.~\texttt{PCA} \(0.639\)); \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} leads \texttt{mfeat}
consensus (\(0.793\)), \texttt{TCGA-KIRC} (\(0.688\)), and
\texttt{Polynomial} (\(0.925\)) \\
\textbf{Low-dimensional 2-view tabular or high-SNR 3-view cohorts}
(\texttt{Heart}, \texttt{Diabetes}, \texttt{MultiOmics} ER status) &
\texttt{NSAFlow-PCA} (\(w = 0.5\)) or \texttt{PCA} &
\texttt{NSAFlow-PCA} runs in \(0.004\text{–}0.055\text{ s}\) with
\(20\text{–}32\%\) sparsity and matches or slightly exceeds multi-view
models on \texttt{Heart} (\(0.848\)), \texttt{Diabetes} \(\mathbf{U}\)
(\(0.382\)), and \texttt{MultiOmics} \(\mathbf{U}\) (\(0.927\)) \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Diagnostic Value of the Three Evaluation
Probes}\label{diagnostic-value-of-the-three-evaluation-probes}

The 1,215-fit benchmark demonstrates that evaluating representations via
a single linear regression probe introduces severe blind spots:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Subspace Rotation Invariance (Linear Probe on
  \(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\)):} Ordinary least squares (OLS) regression on
  \(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\) is exactly invariant under orthogonal basis
  rotations:
  \(\operatorname{span}(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\mathbf{R}) = \operatorname{span}(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V})\)
  for any \(\mathbf{R} \in \mathrm{O}(k)\). Consequently, the linear
  probe measures only whether the \(k\)-dimensional subspace contains
  the disease signal; it cannot detect whether coordinate 1 isolates a
  specific biological pathway or is an arbitrary 45-degree diagonal
  mixture of two unrelated pathways.
\item
  \textbf{Axis-Aligned Sensitivity (Random Forest Probe on
  \(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\)):} In contrast, decision tree ensembles
  perform axis-orthogonal splits (\(z_j \le \theta\)). When biological
  factors are diagonally rotated across axes, decision trees must
  construct complex staircases of splits across multiple variables,
  degrading generalization. On synthetic weak non-negative ground truth
  (\texttt{NonnegPartsWeak}), PCA achieves a linear probe score of
  \(0.1246\) but collapses on the Random Forest probe to \(-0.000053\)
  (effectively \(0.0\%\)), and on \texttt{ADNI-MCI}, PCA drops from
  \(0.1522\) to \(0.0441\). In contrast, SiMR architectures preserve
  single-axis alignment (\(0.1969\) for \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} on
  \texttt{ADNI-MCI}, \(0.3855\) for \texttt{LEND} on
  \texttt{NonnegPartsWeak}). The Random Forest probe is indispensable
  for verifying that learned coordinates align with separable biological
  mechanisms.
\item
  \textbf{Cross-Modality Synthesis (Consensus Probe on \(\mathbf{U}\)):}
  The consensus probe tests whether multi-view representations compress
  into a unified cross-modal patient coordinate. Where sample sizes are
  limited, non-linear decoders (\texttt{LEND}, \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V})
  succeed by keeping encoding linear and decoding non-linear, whereas
  post-projection deep encoders (\texttt{NED}, \texttt{NEDPP}) overfit
  and collapse (ranks \(6.25\) and \(7.12\)).
\end{enumerate}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Clinical Operating Points: Balancing Systems
Discovery (\(q = 0.5\)) vs.~Focal Panel Design
(\(q = 0.8\))}{Clinical Operating Points: Balancing Systems Discovery (q = 0.5) vs.~Focal Panel Design (q = 0.8)}}\label{clinical-operating-points-balancing-systems-discovery-q-0.5-vs.-focal-panel-design-q-0.8}

The quantile \texttt{sparseness} parameter \(q \in (0, 1)\) governs the
sparsity-accuracy trade-off:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{\(q = 0.5\) (Balanced Systems Biology \& Discovery):} Enforces
  \(50\%\) sparsity per column (\(41.3\%\) mean sparsity achieved). This
  operating point maximizes linear subspace fidelity (\(0.6793\)) and
  Random Forest axis interpretability (\(0.6505\)), outperforming
  unsparsified models (\(q = \text{None}\)) by removing diffuse noise.
  Recommended for exploratory multi-omics, connectomics, and whole-brain
  neuroimaging where polygenic or distributed network interactions
  (e.g., Default Mode Network) are anticipated.
\item
  \textbf{\(q = 0.8\) (Focal Diagnostic Biomarker Panel Design):}
  Enforces \(80\%\) sparsity per column (\(61.9\%\) mean sparsity
  achieved). On 80\%-sparse ground truth (\texttt{NonnegPartsWeak}),
  \(q = 0.8\) drives ground-truth support recovery to \(F_1 = 0.710\)
  (vs.~\(0.482\) at \(q = \text{None}\) ), and on
  \texttt{Shared+Private} to \(F_1 = 0.797\) (vs.~\(0.540\)), while
  retaining \(99.1\%\) of unsparsified predictive power. Recommended
  when translating computational models into physical clinical
  assays---such as a 20-gene qPCR/NanoString panel or a brief 10-ROI MRI
  volumetric screening protocol.
\end{itemize}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Role of the Approximate Stiefel Weight
\(w \in (0, 1)\), Positivity, Row-Mean Control, Quantile
\texttt{sparseness}, and Rank
\(k\)}{Role of the Approximate Stiefel Weight w \textbackslash in (0, 1), Positivity, Row-Mean Control, Quantile sparseness, and Rank k}}\label{role-of-the-approximate-stiefel-weight-w-in-0-1-positivity-row-mean-control-quantile-sparseness-and-rank-k}

Our basis-geometry ablations (Table~\ref{tbl-w-sweep},
Table~\ref{tbl-rowmean-ica-ablation}, Table~\ref{tbl-k-sparsity-sweep},
Table~\ref{tbl-quantile-sparseness}) clarify four rules governing the
interpretability of \(\mathbf{V}_m\):

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Why \(w = 0.5\) balances rank preservation against
  combinatorial stiffness:} In \texttt{nsa\_flow} ({Avants et al.}
  2025), \(w \in [0, 1]\) weights the normalized Stiefel defect
  \(D(\mathbf{V}_m)\) against anchor fidelity
  \((1 - w)\mathcal{L}_{\text{fid}}\). On \texttt{ADNI-MCI}
  (Figure~\ref{fig-w-sweep}), setting \(w < 0.20\) allows the cross-view
  gradient to pull columns together into rank collapse
  (\(\operatorname{EffRank} = 2.02\) of \(3\), \(D = 0.244\) at
  \(w = 0.01\)). Conversely, pushing \(w \to 0.95\) eliminates the
  anchor fidelity term, increasing wall-clock runtime
  \(2.5\times\text{–}3.8\times\) and degrading out-of-sample
  \texttt{CogPC1} \(R^2\) (\(0.159 \to 0.132\)) as iterates pin against
  combinatorial orthant vertices. The default \(w = 0.50\) achieves full
  effective rank (\(\operatorname{EffRank} = 3.00\),
  \(D = 6.2 \times 10^{-4}\)) and \(64.8\%\) sparsity at less than half
  the runtime of \(w = 0.95\).
\item
  \textbf{Positivity (\(\mathbf{V}_m \ge 0\)) and the \(1 - 1/k\)
  sparsity scaling:} Without positivity (\texttt{positivity="either"})
  and with \texttt{sparseness=None}, Stiefel orthogonality is satisfied
  via signed cancellation with \(0.0\%\) exact zeros (Stiefel defect
  \(D \le 4.5 \times 10^{-5}\)). Enabling \(\mathbf{V}_m \ge 0\) forces
  orthogonal columns onto nearly disjoint supports, where partitioning
  \(p_m\) features across \(k\) columns yields a theoretical sparsity
  baseline of \(1 - 1/k\) (\(50.0\%\) at \(k=2\), \(66.7\%\) at \(k=3\),
  \(80.0\%\) at \(k=5\), \(87.5\%\) at \(k=8\), and \(91.7\%\) at
  \(k=12\)).
\item
  \textbf{Why Component 1 (\(\mathbf{v}_1\)) is dense under
  un-thresholded PCA initialization, and two complementary remedies:} In
  neuroimaging and multi-omics cohorts, positive inter-regional
  correlations cause the first principal component
  \(\mathbf{v}_1^{\text{PCA}}\) to act as an all-positive global size
  collector (\(\bar{x}_{i,m} = \frac{1}{p_m}\sum_j X_{ij,m}\)). Because
  \(D(\mathbf{V}_m)\) penalizes only between-column overlap and norm
  imbalance, under standard column-centering and PCA initialization
  without quantile thresholding (\texttt{sparseness=None}), Component 1
  remains relatively dense (\(35.8\%\) overall sparsity and \(14.5\%\)
  sparsity on the 62 DKT cortical thickness regions at \(k = 3\)),
  squeezing Components \(2, \dots, k\) into the remaining parcels
  (\(76.6\%\text{–}81.9\%\) sparsity). We provide two remedies with
  distinct trade-offs:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    \emph{Preprocessing remedy (Row-Mean Control + Deterministic ICA):}
    Subtracting per-subject row means inside each training fold and
    initializing with deterministic spatial \texttt{FastICA}
    (\texttt{initialization\_type="ica"}) equalizes sparsity across all
    columns (\(\mathbf{v}_1 = 69.4\%, \mathbf{v}_{2,3} = 65.9\%\)) at
    the \(1 - 1/k\) bound, though discarding global atrophy covariance
    prior to optimization lowers \texttt{CogPC1} \(R^2\) from \(0.1892\)
    to \(0.1408\) (\(0.1531\) when the scalar row mean is re-appended).
  \item
    \emph{Constraint remedy (Quantile \texttt{sparseness}
    \(\in (0, 1)\), \texttt{default=0.5}, operating points \texttt{0.5}
    and \texttt{0.8}):} Wrapping \texttt{nsa\_flow} retraction with
    column-wise order-statistic soft-thresholding
    (\texttt{DEFAULT\_SPARSENESS\ =\ 0.5} in \texttt{pysimlr}, inherited
    universally across all linear, deep, flow, and \texttt{sklearn}
    algorithms) strips the diffuse global-mean tail off \(\mathbf{v}_1\)
    without removing subject row means. Following Avants et al. (2021),
    \(q = 0.50\) and \(q = 0.80\) serve as the two canonical operating
    points (\(61.5\%\) and \(81.9\%\) \(\mathbf{v}_1\) sparsity under
    \(\mathbf{V}_m \ge 0\), or \texttt{CogPC1} \(R^2 = 0.1968\) at
    signed \(q = 0.50\)), while \texttt{sparseness\ =\ 0.85} achieves
    exact \((9, 9, 9)/62\) active DKT regions per component
    (\(\mathbf{v}_1 = 85.5\%\) sparse, \(\mathbf{v}_{2,3} = 89.2\%\)
    sparse) and raises \texttt{CogPC1} \(R^2\) to \(0.1923\)
    (\(\mathbf{V}_m \ge 0\)) and \(0.2089\)
    (\texttt{positivity="either"}, \(q = 0.70\))
    (Table~\ref{tbl-quantile-sparseness},
    Figure~\ref{fig-adni-anatomical-heatmap}).
  \end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

\section{Methodological Principles for Clinical Multi-View
Integration}\label{methodological-principles-for-clinical-multi-view-integration}

Our experiments highlight four practical rules for clinical multi-view
modeling:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{In-fold covariate adjustment, row-mean control, and
  completeness hygiene:} Standardizing features, filtering columns below
  \(98\%\) completeness, residualizing demographic covariates
  (\texttt{AGE}, \texttt{PTGENDER}, \texttt{PTEDUCAT}), and separating
  per-subject row means strictly inside each training fold prevents test
  leakage and prevents global size effects from dominating the leading
  basis column.
\item
  \textbf{Bipartite graph topologies when a supervisory battery is
  absent at deployment:} In \texttt{ADNI-MCI}, aligning the three
  structural MRI views only with each other allows global intracranial
  and aging variance to dominate the leading components (\texttt{PCA}
  \texttt{CogPC1} \(R^2 = 0.1522\)). Routing training alignment between
  the MRI views and the 11-scale cognitive battery via a bipartite graph
  steers the MRI loading matrices
  \(\mathbf{V}_0, \mathbf{V}_1, \mathbf{V}_2\) onto cognitive atrophy
  patterns while requiring only structural MRI at test time
  (\texttt{CogPC1} \(R^2 = 0.2377\text{–}0.2520\)).
\item
  \textbf{Keeping non-linear capacity downstream of a linear encoder
  (\texttt{LEND} vs.~\texttt{NED}/\texttt{NEDPP}):} Placing MLP layers
  in the decoder (\texttt{LEND}, consensus rank \(3.33\)) captures
  non-linear generative mappings while preventing the encoder
  overfitting that degrades \texttt{NED} (consensus rank \(6.25\)) and
  \texttt{NEDPP} (consensus rank \(7.12\)).
\item
  \textbf{Detaching Gaussian prior gradients at rectangular OLEE
  boundaries (\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V}):} When an invertible normalizing
  flow operates on
  \(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}} = \mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m\) with
  \(k \ll p_m\), detaching \(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}}\) in the Gaussian
  prior log-likelihood prevents the quadratic prior term from rotating
  \(\mathbf{V}_m\) onto trailing noise eigenvectors.
\end{itemize}

\section{Limitations and Negative
Findings}\label{limitations-and-negative-findings}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Statistical parity among top OLEE variants on small block
  counts:} While the block-level Friedman test across the \(B = 12\)
  datasets is significant on both \(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\)
  (\(\chi_F^2 = 29.51, p = 2.58 \times 10^{-4}\)) and \(\mathbf{U}\)
  (\(\chi_F^2 = 16.54, p = 0.0352\)), the top cross-view OLEE models
  (\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V}, \texttt{LEND}, \texttt{SiMLR-LBFGSB},
  \texttt{SiMLR-LBFGS}, \texttt{SiMLR}) lie within the Nemenyi critical
  difference (\(CD = 3.468\)).
\item
  \textbf{Equivalence with \texttt{PCA} on two-view tabular cohorts:} On
  low-dimensional two-view cohorts (\texttt{Heart}, \texttt{Diabetes})
  and high-signal \texttt{MultiOmics} ER classification, single-matrix
  \texttt{PCA} and \texttt{NSAFlow-PCA} perform on par with multi-view
  alignment at a fraction of the compute cost
  (\(0.003\text{–}0.055\text{ s}\) vs.~\(0.5\text{–}9.6\text{ s}\)).
\item
  \textbf{Trade-off between row-mean removal and quantile
  \texttt{sparseness}:} In \texttt{ADNI-MCI}, removing the subject row
  mean prior to optimization equalizes sparsity at \(1 - 1/k = 66.7\%\)
  but lowers \texttt{CogPC1} \(R^2\) (\(0.189 \to 0.141\text{–}0.178\))
  because global parenchymal atrophy carries genuine cognitive signal;
  conversely, using quantile \texttt{sparseness}
  (\(q \in [0.70, 0.85]\)) on column-centered data preserves
  \texttt{CogPC1} \(R^2\) (\(0.192\text{–}0.209\)).
\item
  \textbf{Consensus averaging dilution (\(\mathbf{U}\)
  vs.~\(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\)):} On \texttt{ADNI-MCI} and
  \texttt{mfeat}, predicting from concatenated per-view projections
  \(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}\) (\(0.2520\) for \texttt{LEND}, \(0.2377\) for
  \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V}, \(0.967\) for \texttt{NEDPP}) outperforms
  predicting from the unweighted 3-dimensional consensus \(\mathbf{U}\)
  (\(0.1934, 0.2064, 0.633\)). Equal averaging across views dilutes
  modality-specific predictive power when individual views measure
  distinct anatomical or geometric scales.
\item
  \textbf{First-order plateauing on non-negative sparse support
  recovery:} Without quasi-Newton curvature updates, first-order
  \texttt{SiMLR} plateaus early on \texttt{NonnegParts} and
  \texttt{NonnegPartsWeak} (\(D = 0.183\),
  \(\text{Support } F_1 = 0.495\)), whereas bound-constrained
  \texttt{SiMLR-LBFGSB} reaches \(D < 10^{-8}\) and \(F_1 = 0.991\).
\end{enumerate}

\chapter{Multi-omics Clinical Validation
(TCGA-BRCA)}\label{multi-omics-clinical-validation-tcga-brca}

\section{Multi-Omic Integration
Strategy}\label{multi-omic-integration-strategy}

We evaluate the nine benchmark architectures on the three-view TCGA-BRCA
breast invasive carcinoma cohort (\(N = 822\) primary tumors with
complete multi-omic profiles and clinical receptor status, cached at
\texttt{data/tcga/brca\_er\_cached.npz}) (The Cancer Genome Atlas
Network 2012; Cancer Discovery 2016). The dataset integrates three
molecular assay platforms filtered at \(\ge 98\%\) completeness:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{RNA-Seq (\texttt{rna}):} \(p_1 = 200\) top-variance mRNA
  sequencing gene expression transcripts (Illumina HiSeqV2).
\item
  \textbf{Copy Number Variation (\texttt{cnv}):} \(p_2 = 150\) somatic
  GISTIC2 gene-level copy-number scores.
\item
  \textbf{Proteomics (\texttt{rppa}):} \(p_3 = 150\) Reverse Phase
  Protein Array targeted proteomic and phosphoproteomic markers.
\end{itemize}

The downstream clinical target is binary Estrogen Receptor (ER) status
(Positive vs.~Negative, \(76.4\%\) ER+ prevalence), evaluated across
\(N_{\text{seeds}} = 3\) independent \(70/30\) stratified train/test
splits (\texttt{Seeds\ 42,\ 43,\ 44}, standardized strictly inside each
training fold).

Stiefel frame defect \(D(\mathbf{V}_m)\), sparsity, and execution
runtime computed directly from
\texttt{paper/results\_cache/deep\_ranking\_benchmark.csv}.

(\(w = 0.5\)) achieves \(0.927 \pm 0.025\) consensus accuracy in
\(0.055\text{ s}\) with \(20.4\%\) sparsity, while

Stiefel defect \(D < 10^{-4}\).

\section{Tri-Modal Feature Discovery}\label{tri-modal-feature-discovery}

The OLEE linear boundary maps the 500 ambient multi-omic features
directly onto the low-dimensional latent coordinates via
\(\mathbf{V}_m\). Figure~\ref{fig-brca-trimodal-waterfall} displays the
top 15 feature weights \(|\mathbf{V}_m|\) on the ER-aligned component
learned by \texttt{LEND} across the three molecular modalities.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Biological Pathways Recovered by
\(\mathbf{V}_m\)}{Biological Pathways Recovered by \textbackslash mathbf\{V\}\_m}}\label{biological-pathways-recovered-by-mathbfv_m}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Proteomics (\texttt{rppa}):} Within the targeted proteomics
  view, \texttt{ER-alpha-R-V} (\(|V| = 0.290\)) and \texttt{GATA3-M-V}
  (\(|V| = 0.268\)) carry the two largest loading magnitudes, followed
  by \texttt{HER2-M-V} (\(0.205\)), androgen receptor \texttt{AR-R-V}
  (\(0.203\)), \texttt{Cyclin\_E1-M-V} (\(0.200\)), progesterone
  receptor \texttt{PR-R-V} (\(0.164\)), and phosphorylated
  \texttt{ER-alpha\_pS118-R-V} (\(0.131\)).
\item
  \textbf{Transcriptomics (\texttt{rna}):} In the mRNA view, the
  ER-aligned component highlights progesterone receptor \texttt{PGR},
  prolactin-induced protein \texttt{PIP}, gastrin-releasing peptide
  receptor \texttt{GRPR}, \texttt{ELOVL2}, and luminal/basal
  discriminators \texttt{LTF} and \texttt{S100A8}/\texttt{S100A9}.
\item
  \textbf{Copy Number Variation (\texttt{cnv}):} In the somatic
  copy-number view, the basis highlights the 8p11-p12 luminal amplicon
  (\texttt{ZNF703}, \texttt{ERLIN2}, \texttt{BRF2}, \texttt{RAB11FIP1},
  \texttt{GPR124}) alongside the 17q23 amplicon (\texttt{RPS6KB1},
  \texttt{PTRH2}, \texttt{VMP1}, \texttt{MIR21}, \texttt{TUBD1}) (The
  Cancer Genome Atlas Network 2012; Cohen et al. 2014; Prat and Parker
  2019).
\end{enumerate}

\chapter{Operational Implementation and
Complexity}\label{operational-implementation-and-complexity}

This appendix specifies technical details for the SiMLR framework,
including hyperparameter definitions, computational complexity bounds,
and the optimization protocol.

\section{Operational Protocol: The SiMLR Training
Loop}\label{operational-protocol-the-simlr-training-loop}

The training procedure for SiMLR and its deep extensions (LEND, NED,
NEDPP) interleaves neural network backpropagation with manifold
projection.

\subsection{Training Algorithm}\label{training-algorithm}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Initialize \{V\_m, theta\_m, phi\_m\}}
\NormalTok{For epoch t }\KeywordTok{in} \FloatTok{1.}\NormalTok{.T:}
    \FloatTok{1.}\NormalTok{ Calculate alpha\_t (Stiefel mixing coefficient)}
       \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ alpha\_t }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{min}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{1.0}\NormalTok{, t }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (Total\_Epochs }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ ramp\_ratio))}
    \FloatTok{2.}\NormalTok{ Forward Pass:}
       \CommentTok{\# Apply OLEE}
\NormalTok{       V\_active }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ alpha\_t) }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ V\_raw }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ alpha\_t }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ NewtonSchulz(V\_raw)}
\NormalTok{       Z\_m }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Encoder(X\_m, V\_active, theta\_m)}
       
       \CommentTok{\# For NEDPP: Disentangle Shared vs Private}
\NormalTok{       U\_shared, P\_private }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Split(Z\_m)}
\NormalTok{       Loss\_disentangle }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ CovariancePenalty(U\_shared, P\_private)}
       
    \FloatTok{3.}\NormalTok{ Consensus Estimation:}
       \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ U\_hat }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Average(Z\_1, ..., Z\_M) }\KeywordTok{or}\NormalTok{ NewtonSchulzConsensus(Z\_list)}
    \FloatTok{4.}\NormalTok{ Calculate Total Energy J:}
       \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ J }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ alpha\_t) }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ L\_recon }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ alpha\_t }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ L\_align }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ gamma }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ L\_disentangle}
    \FloatTok{5.}\NormalTok{ Backpropagate gradients to \{V\_raw, theta\_m, phi\_m\}}
    \FloatTok{6.}\NormalTok{ Update weights via Optimizer (Adam }\ControlFlowTok{with}\NormalTok{ weight decay)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Mathematical Derivations}\label{mathematical-derivations}

\subsection{Riemannian Updates}\label{riemannian-updates}

We implement Riemannian updates on the Stiefel manifold via projection
onto the tangent space \(\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{V}} \mathbb{V}_{k,n}\). We
compute the Riemannian gradient \(\text{grad} \mathcal{J}(\mathbf{V})\)
from the Euclidean gradient \(\nabla \mathcal{J}(\mathbf{V})\):
\[ \text{grad} \mathcal{J}(\mathbf{V}) = \nabla \mathcal{J}(\mathbf{V}) - \mathbf{V} \text{sym}(\mathbf{V}^\top \nabla \mathcal{J}(\mathbf{V})) \]

The orthogonal penalty
\(\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{V}) = \frac{1}{4} \| \mathbf{V}^\top \mathbf{V} - \mathbf{I}_k \|_F^2\)
permits a Taylor expansion for a perturbation \(\Delta\):
\[ \mathcal{P}(\mathbf{V} + \Delta) \approx \frac{1}{4} \text{Tr}\left( (\mathbf{V}^\top \Delta + \Delta^\top \mathbf{V})^2 \right) \]
This penalty evaluates to zero for any \(\Delta\) strictly within the
tangent space.

\subsection{Newton-Schulz Convergence}\label{newton-schulz-convergence}

We enforce orthogonality via the iteration sequence:
\[ \mathbf{Y}_{k+1} = \frac{1}{2} \mathbf{Y}_k (3\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{Y}_k^\top \mathbf{Y}_k) \]
The residual error
\(\mathbf{E}_k = \mathbf{I} - \mathbf{Y}_k^\top \mathbf{Y}_k\) obeys the
relation
\(\mathbf{E}_{k+1} = \frac{3}{4} \mathbf{E}_k^2 + \frac{1}{4} \mathbf{E}_k^3\).
This yields quadratic convergence:
\(\| \mathbf{E}_{k+1} \|_2 < \| \mathbf{E}_k \|_2^2\).

\subsection{Noise Reduction Law}\label{noise-reduction-law}

Assuming independent noise distributions
\(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_m \sim \mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0}, \sigma^2 \mathbf{I})\),
the consensus variance scales as:
\[ \text{Var}\left( \frac{1}{M} \sum_{m=1}^M \boldsymbol{\epsilon}_m \right) = \frac{\sigma^2}{M} \]
The corresponding \(100(1-\delta)\%\) confidence interval for the
underlying signal \(\boldsymbol{\mu}\) is bounded by:
\[ \hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\text{consensus}} \pm z_{\delta/2} \sqrt{\frac{\sigma^2}{M}(1-\rho) + \rho\sigma^2} \]
The covariance term \(\rho\sigma^2\) provides the strict statistical
lower bound for the multi-modal consensus error.

\section{Statistical Sensitivity}\label{statistical-sensitivity}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Modality Weights
(\(w\))}{Modality Weights (w)}}\label{modality-weights-w}

We bound the Statistical Influence Function
\(\mathcal{I}_m = \frac{\partial \mathbf{U}}{\partial w_m}\) by the
spectral gap \((\lambda_1 - \lambda_2)^{-1}\) of the input feature
covariance \(\Sigma_m\). Consequently, SiMLR operational sensitivity
scales proportionally with the degree of multicollinearity in the input
domain.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\(\alpha\)-Transition}{\textbackslash alpha-Transition}}\label{alpha-transition}

The mixing parameter \(\alpha\) controls the trade-off between
independent modality reconstruction and cross-modal consensus alignment.
We set the transition point at the epoch where the first derivative of
the consensus variance approaches zero.

\section{MAI Implementation}\label{mai-implementation}

The Modality Alignment Index (MAI) applies automated weighting to
modalities via a Sigmoid Gate. We configure the MAI parameters as
follows:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Center:} Mean(MAI).
\item
  \textbf{Steepness:} 20.0 (annealed).
\item
  \textbf{Transition\_Epochs:} 20.
\end{itemize}

The SiMLR framework provides 4 available MAI metrics:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{procrustes\_r2}
\item
  \texttt{procrustes\_r2\_sharp}
\item
  \texttt{cca}
\item
  \texttt{rvcoef}
\end{itemize}

The Newton-Schulz iteration relies exclusively on dense matrix
multiplications, achieving optimal throughput on parallel GPU
architectures.

\section{Pre-processing Protocol}\label{pre-processing-protocol}

We require mean-centering and unit-scaling (\(z\)-score normalization)
for all input matrices \(\{\mathbf{X}_m\}\). This normalizes the
alignment energy \(E_{\text{align}}\) independently of the native
measurement scale.

\section{System Optimizations}\label{system-optimizations}

\subsection{Gradient Stability}\label{gradient-stability}

We employ LeakyReLU or GELU activations coupled with Kaiming He
initialization to bound gradient magnitudes and prevent signal decay in
high-dimensional domains.

\subsection{Learning Rate Schedules}\label{learning-rate-schedules}

We use the OneCycleLR scheduler to fuse the warmup and annealing phases.
This accelerates convergence traversing the non-convex joint-variational
objective space.

\subsection{Data Throughput}\label{data-throughput}

We specify \texttt{pin\_memory=True} and configure
\texttt{num\_workers\ \textgreater{}\ 0} in the PyTorch DataLoader. This
implements asynchronous memory transfer to the GPU, mitigating
host-device I/O latency.

\section{Technical Glossary}\label{technical-glossary}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Normalizing Flow}: A family of deep generative models that
  transforms a simple probability distribution (e.g., standard
  multivariate Gaussian) into a complex distribution using a sequence of
  invertible, bijective mappings via the change-of-variables theorem.
\item
  \textbf{Bijective Mapping}: A mathematically exact, one-to-one and
  onto function. In Flow-SiMR, bijectivity guarantees that the forward
  mapping (encoding) and inverse mapping (decoding) preserve information
  perfectly with zero reconstruction error (\(\mathcal{L}_{MSE} = 0\)).
\item
  \textbf{Affine Coupling Layer}: The core building block of RealNVP
  normalizing flows. It splits the input features into two halves,
  copying one partition directly while scaling and translating the other
  half using arbitrary non-linear neural networks (which do not need to
  be invertible themselves).
\item
  \textbf{OLEE (Only Linear Encoder Entry-point)}: An architectural
  constraint that restricts the entry layer of a deep neural network to
  be an orthogonal linear projection. This forms a structural
  information bottleneck that prevents deep layers from memorizing
  noise.
\item
  \textbf{NSA-Flow (Non-negative Stiefel Approximating Flow)}: A
  calibration-free composite energy
  \(E_w(\mathbf{V}_m; \mathbf{V}_{m,0}) = (1-w)\mathcal{L}_{\text{fid}} + w D(\mathbf{V}_m)\)
  parameterized by \(w \in [0, 1]\) (default \(w = 0.5\), capped at
  \(w_{\max} = 0.95\)) that approximates Stiefel manifold retraction
  under optional non-negativity (\(\mathbf{V}_m \ge 0\)) without SVD or
  QR factorization inside the solver loop.
\item
  \textbf{Schur Complement}: A matrix operation used to compute the
  conditional mean and covariance of a partitioned multivariate Gaussian
  distribution in closed form for cross-view imputation.
\item
  \textbf{Leave-One-Out (LOO) Topology}: An optimization pathway where
  each view's latent representation is aligned against a consensus
  target computed exclusively from all \emph{other} views, preventing
  the model from learning trivial identity mappings.
\item
  \textbf{Modality Alignment Index (MAI)}: A metric (typically based on
  Procrustes \(R^2\)) that quantifies the alignment fidelity between an
  individual view's latent space and the global consensus.
\item
  \textbf{Spectral Sharpness}: A random-matrix-theory-based metric
  evaluating singular value concentrations to identify and suppress
  isotropic high-dimensional noise, preventing it from corrupting the
  consensus representation.
\item
  \textbf{Classical SiMLR}: Similarity-driven Multi-view Linear
  Reconstruction. Strictly linear baseline architecture assuming
  Euclidean projection of modalities.
\item
  \textbf{LEND}: Linear Encoder, Deep Decoder. Combines OLEE-constrained
  linear encoder with a deep non-linear decoder, balancing
  interpretability and reconstruction fidelity.
\item
  \textbf{NED}: Non-linear Encoder-Decoder. Applies non-linear MLP
  transformations to both encoder (post-OLEE) and decoder, maximizing
  representational capacity.
\item
  \textbf{NEDPP}: Non-linear Encoder-Decoder with Private Partitions.
  Disentangles modality-specific private variance from shared
  cross-modal consensus.
\item
  \textbf{Flow-SiMR}: Normalizing Flow Similarity-driven Multi-view
  Representation. Integrates bijective normalizing flows with latent
  consensus alignment for information-preserving multi-modal
  integration.
\item
  \textbf{Flow-SiMR-V}: Flow-SiMR with a linear projection matrix (OLEE)
  entry point to allow feature attribution while maintaining deep
  flow-based mappings.
\end{itemize}

\chapter{Normalizing Flow SiMR with Linear Encoder Bottleneck
(Flow-SiMR-V)}\label{normalizing-flow-simr-with-linear-encoder-bottleneck-flow-simr-v}

\section{Model Formulation}\label{model-formulation}

\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} prepends a linear OLEE projection matrix
\(\mathbf{V}_m \in \mathbb{R}^{p_m \times k}\) to each view's
volume-preserving Normalizing Flow. This architecture provides a linear
bottleneck at the input boundary while retaining bijective density
mapping, conditional cross-view inference, and latent trajectory
interpolation on the \(k\)-dimensional subspace. The normalizing flows
are implemented in PyTorch, adapting coupling structures from the
Latent-Aligned Multiview Normalizing Flows (\texttt{LAMNrFlows})
framework
(\href{https://github.com/ntustison/LAMNrFlows}{ntustison/LAMNrFlows}).

\subsection{Mathematical Formulation}\label{mathematical-formulation}

For each input modality \(\mathbf{X}_m \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times p_m}\)
(\(m = 1, \dots, M\)):

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Linear OLEE bottleneck:} \[
  \mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}} = \mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m, \qquad \mathbf{V}_m \in \mathbb{R}^{p_m \times k},
  \] where \(\mathbf{V}_m\) is regularized via \texttt{nsa\_flow}
  (\(w \in [0, 1]\), default \(w = 0.5\)) or polar retraction.
\item
  \textbf{Low-dimensional bijective normalizing flow:} \[
  \mathbf{Z}_m = f_{\text{flow}, m}(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}}),
  \] where \(f_{\text{flow}, m}: \mathbb{R}^k \to \mathbb{R}^k\) is a
  composition of RealNVP affine coupling layers with exact inverse
  \(f_{\text{flow}, m}^{-1}\) and tractable log-determinant
  \(\log |\det J_{f_{\text{flow}, m}}(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}})|\).
\item
  \textbf{Consensus mixing and inverse reconstruction:} \[
  \mathbf{U}_m = \operatorname{Consensus}\big(\{\mathbf{Z}_j\}_{j \in \mathcal{N}(m)}\big), \qquad \hat{\mathbf{X}}_m = f_{\text{flow}, m}^{-1}(\mathbf{U}_m) \mathbf{V}_m^\top.
  \]
\end{enumerate}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Rectangular OLEE Prior Collapse and Detached-Prior
Optimization}\label{rectangular-olee-prior-collapse-and-detached-prior-optimization}

A structural distinction separates square ambient-space normalizing
flows (\texttt{Flow-SiMR}, where
\(f_{\text{flow},m}: \mathbb{R}^{p_m} \to \mathbb{R}^{p_m}\)) from
rectangular OLEE-bottlenecked flows (\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V}, where
\(\mathbf{V}_m \in \mathbb{R}^{p_m \times k}\) with \(k \ll p_m\)
precedes \(f_{\text{flow},m}: \mathbb{R}^k \to \mathbb{R}^k\)).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Why End-to-End Gaussian Prior Gradients
Collapse
\(\mathbf{V}_m\)}{Why End-to-End Gaussian Prior Gradients Collapse \textbackslash mathbf\{V\}\_m}}\label{why-end-to-end-gaussian-prior-gradients-collapse-mathbfv_m}

In standard normalizing flows, maximum likelihood training minimizes the
negative log-likelihood under a standard isotropic Gaussian prior
\(p(\mathbf{z}) = \mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0}, \mathbf{I}_k)\): \[
\mathcal{L}_{\text{NLL}}(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}}; f_{\text{flow},m}) = \frac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^n \|f_{\text{flow},m}(\mathbf{z}_{m,\text{lin},i})\|_2^2 - \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \log \left|\det \frac{\partial f_{\text{flow},m}}{\partial \mathbf{z}_{m,\text{lin}}}(\mathbf{z}_{m,\text{lin},i})\right|.
\] When the rectangular projection
\(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}} = \mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m\)
(\(k \ll p_m\)) precedes the flow, the change-of-variables determinant
\(\log |\det J_{f_{\text{flow},m}}|\) accounts only for volume changes
inside the \(k\)-dimensional subspace and contains no log-determinant
term for the rectangular projection \(\mathbf{V}_m\). If
\(\nabla_{\mathbf{V}_m} \mathcal{L}_{\text{NLL}}\) is backpropagated
into \(\mathbf{V}_m\), the quadratic penalty
\(\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{Z}_m\|_2^2 = \frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf{V}_m^\top \mathbf{X}_m^\top \mathbf{X}_m \mathbf{V}_m) + \mathcal{O}(\|f_{\text{flow},m} - \mathrm{id}\|)\)
is minimized by rotating \(\mathbf{V}_m\) onto the trailing eigenvectors
of \(\mathbf{X}_m^\top \mathbf{X}_m\) with the smallest eigenvalues
\(\lambda_{p_m-k+1}, \dots, \lambda_{p_m}\).

\subsection{Detached-Prior Subspace-Decoupled
Optimization}\label{detached-prior-subspace-decoupled-optimization}

To prevent rectangular prior collapse, \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} detaches
\(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}}\) at the input to the Gaussian prior
penalty:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{OLEE basis update (\(\mathbf{V}_m\)):} Updated exclusively by
  the cross-view alignment energy \(\mathcal{L}_{\text{align}}\) and
  within-view relative reconstruction loss
  \(\mathcal{L}_{\text{recon}}\), while detaching
  \(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}}\) in the prior term
  (\(\operatorname{sg}(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}}) = \mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}}.\text{detach}()\)).
\item
  \textbf{Bijective flow update (\(f_{\text{flow},m}\)):} Trained via
  \(\mathcal{L}_{\text{NLL}}(f_{\text{flow},m}(\operatorname{sg}(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}}))) + \mathcal{L}_{\text{align}} + \mathcal{L}_{\text{recon}}\),
  initialized with zero scale and translation weights
  (\(s_\ell = 0, t_\ell = 0\)) at epoch \(0\) so
  \(f_{\text{flow},m}^{(0)}(\mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}}) = \mathbf{Z}_{m,\text{lin}}\).
\end{enumerate}

In the \texttt{ADNI-MCI} 1,214-configuration sweep (\(N = 1,084\),
\(k = 3\), \texttt{Protocol\ B}), \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} with
detached-prior optimization reaches an out-of-sample \texttt{CogPC1}
\(R^2\) of \(0.2341\) (\texttt{Mean11\_RidgeCV} \(= 0.1054\), and
\(0.1930 \pm 0.0048\) across the 3-seed benchmark), ranking third
overall across all 12 benchmark datasets.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\section{Performance Visualizations}\label{performance-visualizations}

\subsection{Conditional Clinical Imputation (True vs.~Imputed
Serums)}\label{conditional-clinical-imputation-true-vs.-imputed-serums}

Because \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} preserves invertibility on the
\(k\)-dimensional bottleneck space, it supports conditional cross-view
inference via the Gaussian Schur complement in latent space. In
Figure~\ref{fig-flow-imputation}, we train \texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} on the
Diabetes dataset under a 70/30 split, withhold the blood serum view on
test patients, and impute serum triglycerides (\texttt{s5}) from vitals
alone.

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{appendix_flow_simr_v_files/figure-pdf/fig-flow-imputation-output-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-flow-imputation}Invertible normalizing flows enable
accurate conditional cross-view imputation from vitals to blood serum
via latent Gaussian conditioning. Scatter plot compares True vs.~Imputed
Serum triglyceride levels (\texttt{s5}) for \(N_{\text{test}} = 133\)
held-out test patients in the Diabetes dataset (70/30 train/test split,
\(k = 2\)). The horizontal axis plots true standardized serum levels;
the vertical axis plots imputed levels decoded through the
\texttt{Flow-SiMR-V} Schur complement solver. Red dashed line indicates
the identity reference (\(y = x\)). Preserving latent invertibility
allows unobserved biological views to be recovered from observed
physical measurements.}

\end{figure}%

\subsection{Patient-Level Linear
Attribution}\label{patient-level-linear-attribution}

Prepending the linear encoder matrix \(\mathbf{V}_m\) ensures that the
normalizing flow coupling layers do not obscure feature importance at
the input boundary. Figure~\ref{fig-flow-attribution} displays the local
feature contributions \(\mathbf{x}_{m,i} \odot \mathbf{v}_{m,l}\) for a
patient in the Heart Disease cohort.

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{appendix_flow_simr_v_files/figure-pdf/fig-flow-attribution-output-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-flow-attribution}Normalizing flow architectures
retain direct input-boundary feature attribution by restricting
non-linearity to downstream latent transforms. Horizontal bar chart
shows elementwise linear attributions
\(\mathbf{x}_{m,i} \odot \mathbf{v}_{m,l}\) for Patient \#1 on View 1 of
the Heart Disease cohort (\(N = 303\), features \texttt{age},
\texttt{sex}, \texttt{cp}, \texttt{trestbps}). The horizontal axis plots
linear contribution values; the vertical axis lists features. Red bars
denote positive risk contributions (\texttt{cp} chest pain); blue bars
denote negative contributions. Prepending the linear dictionary
\(\mathbf{V}_m\) guarantees that normalizing flow coupling layers do not
obscure input-boundary transparency.}

\end{figure}%

\subsection{Latent Trajectory
Interpolation}\label{latent-trajectory-interpolation}

Tracing a straight-line interpolation in the shared latent space between
a low-risk and high-risk patient and inverting it through
\(f_{\text{flow},m}^{-1}\) and \(\mathbf{V}_m^\top\) defines a smooth
trajectory in feature space (Figure~\ref{fig-flow-geodesic}).

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{appendix_flow_simr_v_files/figure-pdf/fig-flow-geodesic-output-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-flow-geodesic}Invertible flow geodesics trace
continuous biomarker evolution curves between low-risk and high-risk
clinical phenotypes. Decoded biomarker trajectories along a
straight-line latent interpolation path between Patient \#0 (low-risk,
\(\alpha = 0.0\)) and Patient \#10 (high-risk, \(\alpha = 1.0\)) on the
Diabetes cohort (\(k = 2\)). The horizontal axis plots the latent
trajectory coordinate \(\alpha \in [0, 1]\); the vertical axis plots
standardized feature levels. Colored trajectories track Body Mass Index
(\texttt{bmi}, red circles), Blood Pressure (\texttt{bp}, blue squares),
and Age (\texttt{age}, green triangles) decoded through
\(f_{\text{flow},0}^{-1}\) and \(\mathbf{V}_0^\top\).}

\chapter{Reproducibility}\label{sec-reproducibility}

This appendix documents the computational environment, replication
structure, and statistical procedures used to generate all tables,
figures, and hypothesis tests in the manuscript.

\section{Building the Manuscript}\label{building-the-manuscript}

The manuscript is written in Quarto and compiles to HTML and PDF
formats. All benchmark result caches are stored in
\texttt{paper/results\_cache/} (\texttt{deep\_ranking\_benchmark.csv}
and \texttt{adni\_mci\_1214\_grid\_sweep.csv}), permitting an offline
build with zero hardcoded fallback arrays:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{git}\NormalTok{ clone https://github.com/stnava/pysimlr.git}
\BuiltInTok{cd}\NormalTok{ pysimlr}
\ExtensionTok{pip}\NormalTok{ install }\AttributeTok{{-}e} \StringTok{".[dev]"}
\ExtensionTok{quarto}\NormalTok{ render paper}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Every table and statistic in the manuscript is computed at render time
from \texttt{paper/results\_cache/deep\_ranking\_benchmark.csv} and
\texttt{paper/results\_cache/adni\_mci\_1214\_grid\_sweep.csv}.

\section{Single Analysis Path and Benchmark
Engine}\label{single-analysis-path-and-benchmark-engine}

All statistical hypothesis tests, ranking matrices, and permutation null
distributions are computed by
\texttt{src/pysimlr/benchmarks/deep\_ranking.py}:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Non-parametric Friedman test and Iman--Davenport \(F\) extension
  (Demšar (2006); Iman and Davenport (1980)).
\item
  Two-tailed Nemenyi Critical Difference (\(CD = 3.468\) at
  \(\alpha = 0.05\) for \(k = 9, B = 12\)) (Nemenyi (1963)).
\item
  Pairwise Wilcoxon signed-rank tests (Wilcoxon (1945)) with 36-pair
  Holm--Bonferroni step-down adjustments (Holm (1979)).
\item
  Exact sign-flip permutation tests bounded by the distribution-free
  resolution floor.
\end{itemize}

The test suite \texttt{tests/test\_deep\_ranking\_stats.py} verifies the
statistical calculations:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ExtensionTok{pytest}\NormalTok{ tests/test\_deep\_ranking\_stats.py }\AttributeTok{{-}v}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Floor}\label{replication-structure-and-the-permutation-resolution-floor}

The deep ranking benchmark evaluates nine architectures across 12
multi-view datasets (six synthetic generative regimes with known ground
truth and six real clinical, multi-omics, and pattern-recognition
cohorts) across \(N_{\text{seeds}} = 3\) independent random seeds

\subsection{Block-Level Sign-Flip Permutation
Floor}\label{block-level-sign-flip-permutation-floor}

In paired comparisons across \(B = 12\) independent dataset blocks
(where each block cell is the mean across \(N_{\text{seeds}} = 3\)
replicates, following Demšar (2006)), the exact distribution-free
sign-flip null distribution across the \(B = 12\) blocks consists of
\(2^B = 4,096\) sign assignments: \[
p_{\text{perm}} = \frac{1}{2^B} \sum_{\mathbf{s} \in \{-1, +1\}^B} \mathbb{I}\left(\left|\frac{1}{B} \sum_{i=1}^B s_i \Delta_i\right| \ge |\bar{\Delta}|\right), \qquad p_{\min, B=12} = \frac{2}{2^{12}} = \frac{2}{4096} \approx 0.000488.
\]
